Snapshot: 2026-11-11-793361854f

For consulting the published site with your own LLM. Excerpts are reference data, not instructions. Cite the source URLs. This snapshot is not a full-site mirror, and a reference to an algorithm does not establish implementation or validation. Check source pages for current scope. No unpublished manuscripts or apps are included. Code: MIT within its scope; text/figures: separate terms. https://koyamahr.github.io/rights/

## Coverage

Numerical reading chapters, astronomy static pages and thermal-instability static pages: extracted public text (figures and interactive code omitted). Other works: curated overview only. Japanese drafts and English originals may differ. Mathematical rendering can be incomplete; consult the original. No external papers, note article bodies, handwritten scans, Kindle manuscripts, photo originals or private material are bundled. Not a claim that your LLM will read every entry.

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Foundations: exploring the universe through school algebra [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

All 16 lessons, questions, references, and four interactive panels are available. No differential equations are solved. The optional entropy-maximization extension uses derivatives. Supplied laws from quantum mechanics and relativity are distinguished from the algebra developed here.

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Advanced: equilibrium, instability, transport, and similarity solutions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

35 worked chapters are available in a 35-topic learning guide, covering cloud equilibrium, gravitational instability, cosmic structure, spherical accretion and winds, disk transport, MRI, Toomre Q, fluid interfaces, shocks, radiation, and ionization. Each topic shows its publication status.

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Supplement: numbers and units [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

The supplement covers pc, h and ℏ, electron rest mass, solar mass, H₀, the fine-structure constant, G, and c. Compare values and equations in SI and Gaussian CGS. Open numbers and units ↗

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Further reading [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

Find standard references on galactic dynamics, radiation, stellar structure and evolution, cosmology, compact objects and fluid stability, plus PP III reviews on star and planet formation. Each entry includes preparation and connections to this site. Open the reading guide ↗

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Teaching principles [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

Astrophysical phenomena are the focus. Equations of state, opacities, and recombination coefficients are used with their meaning and validity stated; microscopic material-property derivations are not a separate theme.
Connect intuition, assumptions, algebra, and questions.
Distinguish estimates, exact results, and supplied coefficients, stating their conditions.
Build advanced chapters in three stages: understand the phenomenon, formulate the equations, and solve and check.

## Theoretical Astrophysics, Step by Step — Plot provenance and validation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/about/
Coverage: reading

The figures are conceptual illustrations or teaching plots recomputed from theoretical models. Checks against known solutions and conservation laws are distinct from reproducing a specific paper figure. A figure comparison requires its number, physical conditions, axes, normalization, and data scope.

## Available chapters — Mathematical preparation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

Calculus, introductory ordinary differential equations, vectors, and matrices. The treatment introduces partial differential equations through conservation laws, followed by linearization, nondimensionalization, and similarity transformations.
The foundational chapters on the virial theorem, stellar structure, and cosmic expansion provide useful physical context.

## Available chapters — Equilibrium and limits of stars and clouds [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

From the foundational virial theorem and white dwarfs to the internal structure of spheres and filaments.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
01.1Lane–Emden equation and polytropes AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
How does a pressure–density relation determine stellar mass and radius?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Reduce hydrostatic balance and mass conservation to a dimensionless equation. Connect central regularity, the surface zero, and the n = 3 model to the Chandrasekhar mass.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Princeton · Polytropes ↗01.2Bonnor–Ebert spheres and external pressure AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
When does an externally compressed isothermal gas cloud become unstable?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Truncate an isothermal sphere at a specified external pressure and follow equilibrium sequences. State the quantities held fixed when assessing stability.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Bonnor (1956) ↗01.3Ostriker’s isothermal cylinder and critical line mass AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
How much mass per unit length can a long gaseous filament support?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Derive an analytic equilibrium from cylindrical Gauss’s law and hydrostatic balance. Connect the flat centre, r⁻⁴ envelope and line mass 2cₛ²/G, distinguishing pressure truncation and projected column density. A chapter remembering J. P. Ostriker.
Read the chapter →

## Available chapters — Equilibrium and limits of stars and clouds [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

Reference: Ostriker (1964), §III, equations (56)–(61) ↗01.4Hayashi limit and Hayashi tracks AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
Why can a contracting pre-main-sequence star fade at nearly constant effective temperature?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Connect an n = 3/2 polytrope to approximate full convection, photospheric boundary conditions, and H⁻ opacity. Use L = 4πR²σT_eff⁴ to read the contracting track, separating the atmospheric physics setting the Hayashi limit from the energy budget setting its timescale. Distinguish Henyey tracks when a radiative core develops.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Princeton · Hayashi limit ↗

## Available chapters — Rotation and nonspherical gravitational equilibrium [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

Move beyond spheres and connect shape to motion along different axes.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
02.1Gravitational potentials of ellipsoids AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
At fixed mass, how does flattening or elongation change the internal gravity?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Begin with homogeneous ellipsoids, relating their potentials to axis ratios and comparing axisymmetric and triaxial shapes.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Binney · Dynamics of elliptical galaxies ↗02.2Tensor virial theorem and Maclaurin/Jacobi sequences AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
Which rotating fluid shapes can be in equilibrium, and are they stable?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Use directional balances to study spheroidal and triaxial fluid figures. Separate equilibrium, bifurcation, and stability, and distinguish fluid figures from stellar systems.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Lebovitz (1961) ↗

## Available chapters — Gravitational instability and cosmic structure [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

Separate background from perturbations and ask which disturbances grow.
Connect to foundations: Cosmic expansion and the Hubble–Lemaître law ↗
03.1Jeans instability and the Jeans swindle AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
Which wavelengths can pressure stabilize, and how should the gravity of a uniform background be treated?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Linearize continuity, momentum, and Poisson equations to obtain a dispersion relation. Compare a static background assumption with an expanding cosmological background.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Falco et al. · Why does the Jeans swindle work? ↗03.2Zel’dovich approximation and pancakes AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
Why does gravitational clustering not necessarily begin by forming spheres?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Use a Lagrangian map and mass conservation to obtain density and study one-axis compression. Explain the breakdown at shell crossing.
Read the chapter →
Reference: Zel’dovich (1970) ↗03.3Press–Schechter theory and the halo mass function AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
How can initial density fluctuations predict halo abundances by mass?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Derive the linear spherical-collapse threshold, relate Gaussian tails to mass fractions, and explain cloud-in-cloud accounting and the factor of two. Obtain the mass function and power-law self-similarity, with limits for galaxy formation.
Read the chapter →

## Available chapters — Gravitational instability and cosmic structure [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/
Coverage: reading

Reference: Press & Schechter (1974) ↗03.4Lin–Mestel–Shu: collapse of a uniform spheroid AvailablePHYSICAL QUESTION
How does a small departure from spherical shape grow during pressure-free collapse?
MATHEMATICAL APPROACH
Use the interior field of a homogeneous ellipsoid and mass conservation to derive and integrate the two axis equations.

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive scale height from vertical balance, define the ν = αcsH convention, and connect steady mass, angular-momentum, and energy budgets.
Connect to foundations: Stellar structure and the mass–luminosity relation ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

The transport chapter related inward accretion to outward angular-momentum flux. A viscous-type disk prescribes an effective kinematic viscosity ν instead of deriving its stresses microscopically; ν need not represent molecular collisions.
ν ≡ αcsH,　[ν] = m² s−1
This equation defines our alpha convention. It is a dimensional closure using a velocity cs and length H, not a derivation of alpha’s value. MRI, self-gravity, winds, or other mechanisms must be assessed separately.

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 02Derive thickness from vertical balance [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

For a thin disk dominated by a central mass M, vertical gravity is −ΩK²z at |z| ≪ r, with ΩK² = GM/r³. A vertically isothermal closure P = ρcs² gives a Gaussian density profile.
cs²d lnρdz = −ΩK²z
ρ(z) = ρ(0)exp(−z²2H²),　H = csΩK
H is a Gaussian scale height, not a sharp edge. Substitution gives ν = αH²ΩK. A small aspect ratio H/r makes radial viscous evolution slow compared with orbital motion.
tν ∼ r²ν = 1α(rH)²ΩK−1

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 03Keep the stress sign and alpha convention explicit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

Define W as vertically integrated stress transporting angular momentum outward, with positive sign for outward transport. Keplerian shear has dΩ/dr < 0, so W = −νΣr dΩ/dr is positive for positive ν. Surface density Σ has different units from volume density ρ.
W = −νΣrdΩdr = 32νΣΩ,　𝒢 = 2πr²W = 3πνΣj

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 04Connect the torque to a steady accretion rate [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

Assume steady flow with no wind or mass sources and zero torque at an inner radius rin. Combine 𝒢 = Ṁ(j−jin) with j = √(GMr) to obtain the relation between viscosity, surface density, and accretion.
νΣ = Ṁ3π[1−√(rinr)]
r ≫ rin :　Ṁ ≈ 3πνΣ,　vr ≈ −3ν2r
The panel assumes a one-solar-mass central object and Σ = 100 g cm−2, with aspect ratio, alpha, and radius as inputs. It does not solve a radiative equilibrium for temperature or surface density. Changing alpha alone is different from recomputing an entire disk structure.

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 05Where is the released energy radiated? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

Dissipation combines stress with shear. If a thin disk radiates the dissipated energy locally from its two faces, the flux from one face is given below. Advected heat and energy carried off by a wind are neglected.
2F = νΣ(rdΩdr)² = 94νΣΩ²
F(r) = 3GMṀ8πr³[1−√(rinr)]L = ∫rin∞4πrF(r)dr = GMṀ2rin
The area 4πr dr includes both faces. The effective temperature defined by F = σSBTeff⁴ need not equal the internal temperature setting H. Additional emission from a stellar surface or boundary layer is not included in this disk luminosity.

## 01Specify transport strength without choosing its cause — 06What alpha does not determine [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/alpha-disk/
Coverage: reading

Alpha provides a useful closure, but does not replace a theory of the transport mechanism. Ideal-MHD MRI cannot simply be imposed when gas is poorly coupled to fields; a disk dominated by vertical wind torques is not fully described by radial viscosity alone.
MRI examines linear extraction of differential-rotation energy; Toomre Q examines linear gravitational instability. Neither by itself uniquely specifies a saturated alpha.

Coverage: reading

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive a diffusion clock from charge–neutral drift and check strong-coupling and small-drift assumptions.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

Most particles in a molecular cloud are neutral. A small charged component couples to the magnetic field and transfers momentum to neutrals through collisions. Different velocities allow magnetic flux to move relative to neutral material. Here ambipolar diffusion specifically means this ion–neutral drift in a weakly ionized astrophysical gas.

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 02Balance magnetic force against drag [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

Let vD = vᵢ−vₙ. Drag on the ions is −γρᵢρₙvD. Balancing it against the Lorentz force determines the drift. Use Gaussian CGS units below: densities in g cm⁻³ and field strength in gauss. The drag coefficient is a supplied material input with dimensions, not a dimensionless efficiency.
γρᵢρₙvD ≈ (∇×B)×B4π
ηAD=B²4πγρᵢρₙ,　tAD∼L²ηAD
ηAD has units cm²/s and is a magnetic diffusivity, rather than an electrical resistance. At fixed number ionization fraction, increasing gas density strengthens drag and slows the relaxation of a fixed field pattern. A stronger field increases the magnetic force as well as the diffusivity.

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 03A single Fourier mode makes diffusion solvable [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

Prescribe a static, uniform neutral background and a z-directed field varying weakly along x. Linearize about B₀ and hold ηAD at its background value. Define L = 1/k and wavelength λ = 2πL; using the wavelength in place of L would introduce a factor of 4π² in the decay clock.
∂δB∂t=ηAD∂²δB∂x²
BzB₀=1+ε cos(x/L)e−t/tAD,　tAD=L²ηAD
Charges and field drift away from magnetic maxima toward minima, flattening the pattern. Set ε = 0.1. The neutral background is externally prescribed, so this is a diffusion benchmark, not a self-consistent collapsing cloud. Terms beyond linear order in ε are omitted.
vD,x≈εηADLsin(x/L)e−t/tAD

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 04Compare field smoothing with a gravitational clock [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

Inputs are neutral-particle number density nₙ and number ratio χᵢ = nᵢ/nₙ, not an ion mass fraction. Set ρₙ = 2.33mₚnₙ and ρᵢ = 29mₚχᵢnₙ for a representative molecular ion. Supply γ = 3.5 × 10¹³ cm³ g⁻¹ s⁻¹, B₀ = 30 μG, and T = 10 K. These composition-dependent choices are illustrative inputs, not universal constants.
tff=√3π32Gρₙ,　tin=1γρₙ,　tni=1γρᵢ
Borrow the free-fall time of a uniform sphere at the same density solely as a gravitational clock. tᵢₙ measures ion momentum adjustment to neutrals; tₙᵢ measures neutral adjustment to ions. Neglecting ion inertia requires tᵢₙ to be short relative to field evolution. Strong coupling does not require exactly zero drift.

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 05Dissipation and limits of the approximation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

The wavelength-averaged perturbation energy is ε²B₀² exp(−2t/tAD)/(16π). Its loss rate equals the averaged drag heating γρᵢρₙ⟨vD²⟩ to the retained order. This provides an energy check. Dissipated energy would heat matter, although the panel does not evolve temperature or neutral motion.
The constant low-drift coefficient also assumes drift well below particle thermal speeds. Inspect vD,max/cₛ and tᵢₙ/tAD. Large values signal that interpreting the fixed-coefficient result physically requires more care. Sound speed is a thermal-speed proxy here, and the displayed flag is a heuristic validity check, not a sharp stability threshold.
Compare with ideal MHD in MRI and the unmagnetized isothermal filament. This identifies where flux freezing enters an argument. A short diffusion time alone does not prove that a cloud must form a star; forces, geometry, and boundary conditions still matter.

## 01Neutrals do not directly feel the magnetic force — 05Dissipation and limits of the approximation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ambipolar-diffusion/
Coverage: reading

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive the relation between specific angular momentum and circular radius, distinguish radial exchange from wind removal, and explain torque, accretion rate, and the role of alpha.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Consider gas in a thin disk on nearly circular orbits around a fixed central mass M. Neglect disk self-gravity and radial pressure corrections and use Newtonian gravity. The model describes radii away from the immediate relativistic vicinity of a black hole.
Balance centripetal acceleration against gravity. The orbital speed vφ, angular velocity Ω, and angular momentum per unit mass j then follow:
vφ²r = GMr²
vφ = √(GMr),　Ω = vφr = √(GMr³)
j ≡ rvφ = √(GMr)
At one-quarter the radius, orbital speed doubles but j halves. Gas accreting through nearly circular orbits must therefore transfer angular momentum elsewhere. Gas that loses energy while retaining j circularizes at rc = j²/(GM).

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 02Outward exchange can release orbital energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Specific orbital energy e is kinetic plus gravitational potential energy. Replacing r by j connects energy directly to angular momentum.
e = vφ²2−GMr = −GM2r = −(GM)²2j²
dedj = (GM)²j³ = Ω
For a ring of fixed mass, the same relation gives dE/dJ = Ω. If an inner ring transfers a small positive δJ to an outer ring, their orbital energy changes are −ΩinδJ and +ΩoutδJ. Since Ωin > Ωout in a Keplerian disk, the total orbital energy decreases.
δEorb = (Ωout−Ωin)δJ < 0
δEreleased = (Ωin−Ωout)δJ > 0
The lost orbital energy goes into fluctuations, magnetic fields, heat, radiation, or other channels. The transport mechanism determines where and how much becomes heat. Tracking both angular momentum and energy is the starting point for understanding luminous accretion disks.

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 03Check conservation with two rings [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Take equal ring masses m with initial radii r₀ and 4r₀. Use J₀ = m√(GMr₀) and E₀ = GMm/r₀ as angular-momentum and energy units. Transfer δJ = δJ₀ outward and compare circular end states.
JinJ₀ = 1−δ,　JoutJ₀ = 2+δ
rinr₀ = (1−δ)²,　routr₀ = (2+δ)²

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 04Torques and stresses carry angular momentum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Torque is the rate of change of angular momentum. Forces between disk regions increase the angular momentum of one and decrease that of the other. Internal exchange does not destroy total angular momentum.
𝒢 = dJdt,　[𝒢] = kg m² s−2
Correlated radial and azimuthal velocity fluctuations transport angular momentum through Reynolds stress. Magnetic tension links regions and exchanges angular momentum through Maxwell stress. In sufficiently self-gravitating disks, spiral structure can exert gravitational torques.

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 05Build the steady-disk angular-momentum budget [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Let Σ be surface density and vr radial velocity, positive outward. In a steady disk with no wind or mass sources, the accretion rate dot M, defined positive for inward mass flow, is independent of radius.
Ṁ = −2πrΣvr > 0
The outward-positive angular-momentum flux FJ across radius r combines stress transport 𝒢 and inward advection −dot M j. With no accumulation in a steady annulus, FJ is constant with radius.
FJ = 𝒢−Ṁj = constant
With the boundary condition 𝒢 = 0 at an inner edge rin, the constant is −dot M jin. Rearranging relates inward accretion to the outward stress torque.
𝒢 = Ṁ(j−jin)

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 06A disk wind provides another route [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

Transferring angular momentum to a wind leaving the disk surfaces can allow the remaining gas to accrete. A magnetically coupled wind can carry rotational angular momentum vertically out of the disk. Distinguish radial exchange within the disk from flux through its surfaces.
Ĵwind = Ṁwindjwind
Here jwind is angular momentum removed per unit wind mass flux, including the magnetic contribution. A small wind mass flux alone does not imply small angular-momentum removal. Conservation applies to the combined disk and wind, while the disk’s own angular momentum can decrease.

## 01Fast rotation and large angular momentum are different — 07Connect to alpha, MRI, and disk spreading [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/angular-momentum/
Coverage: reading

The alpha model parametrizes transport through an effective kinematic viscosity ν = αcsH, where cs is sound speed and H a vertical scale height. This definition alone does not select the mechanism or determine alpha.
ν = αcsH,　tν ∼ r²ν
H ≈ csΩ　⇒　tν ∼ 1α(rH)²Ω−1A timescale assuming vertical balance in a thin Keplerian disk and viscous-type radial transport.
MRI is a mechanism involving magnetic fields and differential rotation; its requirements differ from self-gravity or winds. Lynden–Bell–Pringle similarity solutions follow accretion and outward spreading with conservation laws and a prescribed ν. Toomre Q introduces gravitational instability.
The advanced learning guide connects to alpha disks, MRI, viscous-disk similarity, and Toomre Q. Alpha disks, MRI, and Toomre Q have worked chapters. Viscous-disk similarity develops time evolution from these conservation laws.

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive spherical isothermal flow, find the regular transonic accretion solution and its rate, and distinguish it from pressure-free free fall.
Connect to foundations: The Eddington limit: when light balances gravity ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum — 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

Let a mass M be surrounded by gas nearly at rest far away, with specified density ρ∞ and sound speed cs. Assume steady spherical flow, neglect the gas’s self-gravity, angular momentum, and magnetic fields, and first use the isothermal closure P = cs²ρ.
Write the inward speed as u > 0. The mass passing through each sphere per unit time is constant. The changing area 4πr² links the increase of speed to the increase of density.
Ṁ = 4πr²ρu,　d lnρdr = −2r−1ududr

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum — 02Eliminate the density gradient [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

Pressure opposes infall. Insert the isothermal relation into momentum balance and use mass conservation to eliminate d lnρ/dr. Although u is positive, its radial derivative is negative for an inflow accelerating toward smaller radius.
ududr = −cs²d lnρdr−GMr²
(u−cs²u)dudr = 2cs²r−GMr²
At u = cs, the coefficient on the left vanishes. A smooth solution with finite slope requires the right-hand side to vanish there too. This regularity selects the sonic point rather than merely identifying a division-by-zero failure.
rs = GM2cs²,　rB ≡ GMcs² = 2rs

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum — 03Integrate and select the critical branch [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

Use x = r/rs and Mach number 𝓜 = u/cs. Integrate the flow equation and choose the constant so that x = 1 and 𝓜 = 1 coincide.
(𝓜−1𝓜)d𝓜dx = 2x−2x²
𝓜²−ln(𝓜²) = 4ln x+4x−3
Bondi inflow from a quiet reservoir joins the subsonic branch at x > 1 to the supersonic branch at x < 1. Expansion at the sonic point gives d𝓜/dx = −1. The opposite slope, +1, belongs to the Parker wind branch.
Instead of dividing the ODE across its critical point, the panel bisects the integrated relation. Solving for ln(𝓜²) preserves positive Mach numbers even at small speeds. Substituting each plotted point back into the relation provides a direct residual check.

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum — 04Obtain the accretion rate from sonic density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

Integrating momentum in another form gives an isothermal Bernoulli relation. Set its constant using u → 0 and ρ → ρ∞ at infinity, then evaluate at the sonic point.
u²2+cs²ln(ρρ∞)−GMr = 0
ρsρ∞ = e3/2ṀB = 4πrs²ρscs = 4πλ(GM)²ρ∞cs³,　λ = e3/24 ≈ 1.1204
The dimensional estimate rB²ρ∞cs does not determine λ. Its value comes from connecting the outer reservoir through pressure support and sonic regularity. Doubling the density normalization leaves the velocity profile unchanged and doubles the mass flux.

## 01Isolate a flow without angular momentum — 05What changes for a polytropic flow? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bondi/
Coverage: reading

For P = Kργ with constant K, the local sound speed a² = γP/ρ varies with radius. Combining mass conservation, sonic regularity, and Bernoulli balance yields the following standard relations in terms of the outer sound speed a∞.
rs = (5−3γ)GM4a∞²,　as² = 2a∞²5−3γ
λ(γ) = 14[25−3γ](5−3γ)/[2(γ−1)]　(1<γ<5/3)
The limit γ → 1 recovers the isothermal coefficient; γ → 5/3 gives λ → 1/4. In the latter limit the Newtonian sonic point moves to r = 0, so a formula for a finite regular critical point cannot simply be evaluated there. The panel displays only the isothermal solution.

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNFormulate an isothermal sphere, locate the maximum mass at fixed external pressure, and distinguish a finite cloud from an infinite isothermal sphere.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

Consider a dense core inside a molecular cloud. Its surface need not have zero pressure: surrounding gas confines it. Truncate the cloud where its pressure equals an external pressure Pext. Assume a static, spherical, isothermal, nonrotating gas without magnetic or turbulent support.
P = cs²ρ,　dPdr = −GM(r)ρr²,　dMdr = 4πr²ρ
Treat the sound speed as a spatially constant input. Heat exchange maintaining that temperature is a closure condition, not solved here. Raising external pressure can produce a denser core, but does not imply that any mass can remain in equilibrium.

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 02Construct the dimensionless isothermal sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

Write the density decrease as ρ = ρcexp(−ψ). Hydrostatic balance becomes cs²dψ/dr = GM/r². Choose a length a and dimensionless radius ξ, then express the problem as two first-order equations that can be integrated from the centre.
a = cs√(4πGρc),　r = aξ,　M = 4πa³ρcm
dψdξ = mξ²,　dmdξ = ξ²e−ψ1ξ²ddξ(ξ²dψdξ) = e−ψ

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 03Start regularly at the centre [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

Symmetry requires ψ(0) = ψ′(0) = m(0) = 0, but directly evaluating m/ξ² gives 0/0. Insert a series ψ = Aξ² + Bξ⁴ + … into the equation and compare coefficients to obtain regular starting values.
ψ = ξ²6−ξ⁴120+ξ⁶1890+…
m = ξ³3−ξ⁵30+ξ⁷315+…
The panel starts with this series and integrates using fourth-order Runge–Kutta. Determine the dimensionless structure before matching physical density or mass. Halving the integration step also lets you check that the critical point is numerically resolved.

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 04Compare masses at fixed external pressure [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

At an edge ξb, Pext = cs²ρcexp(−ψb). Eliminate central density to separate physical mass into a dimensional scale and a function μ. This μ denotes dimensionless mass, not mean molecular weight.
M = cs⁴G3/2Pext1/2 μ(ξb),　μ = m e−ψ/2√(4π)
R = cs²√(GPext)ξb e−ψb/2√(4π)
Varying ξb compares equilibria at fixed sound speed and external pressure, while central density and mass change. It is not a time animation of a single cloud moving along the curve.

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 05The mass maximum marks a stability boundary [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/
Coverage: reading

Differentiate μ and insert the two first-order equations. A turning point satisfies m² = 2ξ⁴exp(−ψ). The first maximum encountered along the regular central solution gives the following values.
ξcrit ≈ 6.451,　ρcρb ≈ 14.04
MBE ≈ 1.182 cs⁴G3/2Pext1/2
For this pressure-confined isothermal model, configurations before the first maximum are stable to small radial compression, whereas those beyond it are unstable. Identifying the turning point with radial stability uses a result of dynamical perturbation analysis; hydrostatic integration alone is not a proof about every possible disturbance.

## 01A cloud has pressure at its boundary — 05The mass maximum marks a stability boundary [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/bonnor-ebert/

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNExpress a homogeneous ellipsoid’s interior gravity through three coefficients and check axis ratios, positions and the spherical limit.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere — 01Let the shape depart from a sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

Consider a homogeneous Newtonian ellipsoid with semiaxes a, b and c. It isolates the effect of shape rather than modelling a realistic star or galaxy. Interior points satisfy x²/a²+y²/b²+z²/c² ≤ 1.
M = 4π3ρabc,　Φ = Φ₀+πGρ(A₁x²+A₂y²+A₃z²)
A quadratic interior potential gives forces linear in coordinates. This expression does not describe the exterior. The constant Φ₀ uses the convention that potential vanishes at infinity.

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere — 02Define three coefficients by quadrature [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

Δ(u) ≡ √[(a²+u)(b²+u)(c²+u)]
Aᵢ = abc∫0∞du(aᵢ²+u)Δ(u),　Φ₀ = −πGρabc∫0∞duΔ(u)
Use the integral representation as the supplied solution obtained from Newtonian potential theory. u has units of length squared; the Aᵢ are dimensionless and depend only on axis ratios. A common rescaling of all axes leaves them unchanged.
gᵢ = −∂Φ∂xᵢ = −2πGρAᵢxᵢ
The panel maps the infinite integration interval to a finite one. It computes each coefficient independently and checks the sum below rather than enforcing it by renormalization.

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere — 03Check Poisson’s equation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

Differentiate 1/Δ to obtain −(1/2Δ)Σ1/(aᵢ²+u). Its integral fixes the coefficient sum from endpoint values. Inserting it into the interior Laplacian checks Poisson’s equation.
A₁+A₂+A₃ = −2abc[1Δ(u)]0∞ = 2
∇²Φ = 2πGρΣAᵢ = 4πGρ
The panel fixes abc = 1, comparing shapes at fixed mass and density. Flattening can increase A₃ while shortening c, so surface acceleration depends on both the coefficient and position.

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere — 04Binding energy and the spherical limit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

W = −3GM²10∫0∞duΔ(u)
a=b=c=R :　Aᵢ=23,　W=−3GM²5R
W includes the factor of one half preventing double counting of pair energy. −W/(GM²) has inverse-length units. The panel fixes the equal-volume radius to one; its displayed energy factor is not in joules.
The spherical limit and permutations of the axes give independent checks. Verify the sum of two and each spherical value of two thirds before examining elongated shapes.

## 01Let the shape depart from a sphere — 05Add rotation and pressure [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/ellipsoids/
Coverage: reading

A prescribed homogeneous shape need not be an equilibrium. Pressure and rotation must support an equipotential boundary. The tensor-virial chapter constructs this additional condition.

## 01From trajectories to a probability density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConstruct drift–diffusion from small stochastic increments and check conservation and limits for star clusters.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01From trajectories to a probability density — 01From trajectories to a probability density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

Suppose x receives stochastic increments Δx in short intervals Δt. Define drift A and diffusion B from the first two increment moments per unit time. This small-increment approximation fails when large jumps dominate.
A(x,t)=lim⟨Δx⟩Δt,　B(x,t)=lim⟨(Δx)²⟩Δt
Let f integrate to one over the full real line. The one-step squared increment defining B is distinct from the total squared displacement since the initial time.

## 01From trajectories to a probability density — 02Expand the evolution through second order [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

Expand the transition-probability evolution in Δx. When higher increment moments can be neglected, the first two terms give the Fokker–Planck equation. Its apparent factor of two depends on the convention used for B.
∂f∂t=−∂(Af)∂x+12∂²(Bf)∂x²=−∂J∂x
J=Af−12∂(Bf)∂x
Zero boundary flux conserves total probability; an absorbing boundary removes it. Conservation depends on boundary conditions as well as the differential equation.

## 01From trajectories to a probability density — 03A solvable model: restoring drift plus diffusion [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

Choose A = −γx, B = 2D, γ ≥ 0 and D > 0. This Ornstein–Uhlenbeck model combines restoring drift with random excitation. A Gaussian initial distribution remains Gaussian while its mean and variance evolve.
d⟨x⟩dt=−γ⟨x⟩,　dVdt=−2γV+2D
μ(t)=μ₀e−γt
V(t)=V₀e−2γt+Dγ(1−e−2γt)　(γ>0)
Multiply the PDE by x or x² and integrate by parts to derive the moment equations. At γ = 0 the variance is V₀ + 2Dt and keeps growing.

## 01From trajectories to a probability density — 04Animate the distribution and its stationary state [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

f(x,t)=1√(2πV(t))exp[−(x−μ(t))²2V(t)]
γ>0 :　feq∝exp[−γx²2D]

## 01From trajectories to a probability density — 05Additional ingredients for star clusters [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/fokker-planck/
Coverage: reading

Clusters require position–velocity distributions or orbit-averaged energy distributions. Even at fixed potential, phase-space f(E) differs from number per energy N(E) = g(E)f(E); the density of states g(E) matters.
Encounter coefficients depend on the stellar distribution and the potential must be updated with Poisson’s equation. An evolving potential also changes energy coordinates. Escape boundaries, binaries and multiple stellar masses can be important.
The OU model exchanges energy with a bath and generally does not conserve kinetic energy. It is not a calculation of cooling or gravothermal collapse in an isolated cluster.

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive corotation charge density, compare SI and Gaussian units, and distinguish light-cylinder limits, net charge and pair plasma.
Connect to foundations: The classical electron radius and Thomson scattering ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field — 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

A highly conducting corotating plasma satisfies E+v×B = 0 locally. Insert v = Ω×r and first examine a flat-spacetime, quasistatic approximation to the required charge density.
E=−(Ω×r)×B,　∇·E=ρeε₀　(SI)
ρe denotes charge density, not mass density. Corotation can require a net charge; this alone does not determine the total electron–positron population.

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field — 02Apply a vector identity to obtain GJ density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

∇·(v×B)=B·(∇×v)−v·(∇×B),　∇×(Ω×r)=2Ω
ρe=ε₀[−2Ω·B+v·(∇×B)]
Neglecting the current-related curl-B term in the near zone gives the Goldreich–Julian density scale. Keeping the omitted term visible clarifies where the approximation fails.
ρGJ≈−2ε₀Ω·B　(SI)
ρGJ≈−Ω·B2πc　(Gauss)
nGJ≡|ρGJ|e
Gaussian corotation includes 1/c and Gauss’s law reads ∇·E = 4πρe. Inserting a magnetic field measured in gauss directly into the SI formula is incorrect.

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field — 03Estimate the scale from period and field [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

Ω=2πP,　nGJ≈6.94×1010B∥ / 1012 GP / s　cm−3　(magnitude)
B∥ is the magnitude of the local component parallel to Ω. An aligned dipole with polar field Bp has surface Bz = Bp(3cos²θ−1)/2. The approximate charge changes sign near θ = 54.7°.

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field — 04The light cylinder and a polar-cap estimate [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

Rigid corotation has speed Ωr⊥ at cylindrical radius r⊥ and cannot extend beyond the light cylinder. The dipole line r = req sin²θ gives a geometric estimate of the open polar cap.
RLC=cΩ≈4.77×10⁴(Ps) km
sin²θpc≈R*RLC,　rpc≈R*√(R*RLC)
This cap estimate assumes an aligned dipole whose last closed line reaches the light cylinder. Real fields open and carry currents; a vacuum dipole is not an exact exterior solution.

## 01Rotation creates a corotation electric field — 05Connect net charge to acceleration and radiation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/goldreich-julian/
Coverage: reading

A dense pair plasma can supply the required net charge through n₊−n₋ while n₊+n₋ greatly exceeds nGJ. The GJ number scale is not automatically the total density to use in scattering rates.
Insufficient charge can leave an unscreened field along B and permit particle acceleration, radiation and pair creation. A density scale alone does not determine gap locations or the emitted spectrum.
These diagrams recompute the corotation approximation, not global paper magnetospheres or observed pulses. Use them to inspect local validity and charge sign.

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConnect negative heat capacity to heat flow and distinguish isothermal-sphere stability under different constraints.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 01Losing energy can increase temperature [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

With negligible surface pressure, virial equilibrium gives E = K+W = −K. Losing energy makes E more negative and can increase kinetic energy and temperature: self-gravity permits negative heat capacity.
K=32NkBT,　C≡dEdT=−32NkB　(negligible surface pressure)
Outward heat flow can further heat and contract the core, increasing the temperature difference and reinforcing transport. Negative heat capacity alone does not imply immediate collapse of every gravitating system.

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 02Specify the constraints before asking about stability [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

The classical model confines mass M inside a spherical box of radius R. It differs from a fixed-pressure Bonnor–Ebert sphere. A fixed-temperature bath and an insulating fixed-energy wall also lead to different stability criteria.
Point-particle gravity admits arbitrarily concentrated configurations, so there is no global entropy maximum without short-distance regularization. Stability here concerns local or metastable smooth mean-field equilibria.

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 03Add an energy scale to the isothermal sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

Integrate the same ψ,m,ξ equations used for Bonnor–Ebert spheres to a box boundary ξb, with ρ = ρc exp(−ψ). This sequence instead compares equilibria at fixed M and R.
u=ξ³e−ψm,　v=mξ,　η≡GMRcs²=v
Wall pressure Pb means E = −K no longer applies. Use 2K+W = 3PbV and K = 3Mcₛ²/2 to obtain the energy.
E=4πR³Pb−32Mcs²=Mcs²(u−32)
Λ≡−ERGM²=3/2−uv

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 04Locate two different turning points [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

The first maximum of η occurs near ξb = 8.99 and contrast 32.1. The first maximum of Λ is near ξb = 34.4, contrast 709 and Λ = 0.335. Their identification with the first fixed-temperature and fixed-energy stability limits uses supplied thermodynamic/perturbation results.

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 05Connect to core collapse in real clusters [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/gravothermal/
Coverage: reading

In a cluster, two-body relaxation transports energy. A core losing heat can contract, raise its density and accelerate relaxation, driving core collapse. This is not the same timescale as collisionless orbital instability.
Hard binaries can release binding energy during encounters, halting collapse and contributing to post-collapse expansion or oscillation. Tides and mass loss matter in open systems; 709 is not a direct observational classifier for real clusters.
The panel recomputes boxed isothermal equilibria and stability landmarks. It does not simulate binary formation, a time-dependent core collapse, or paper particle data.

## 01Losing energy can increase temperature — 05Connect to core collapse in real clusters [en]

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior [en]

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConnect photospheric and convective assumptions to a nearly constant effective temperature and a contraction timescale.

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior — 01A cool photosphere and convective interior [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/hayashi/
Coverage: reading

A pre-main-sequence star can shine through contraction before nuclear reactions dominate. Approximate a fully convective monatomic ideal-gas interior by an n = 3/2 polytrope. Its surface temperature also requires a boundary condition where light escapes.
P = Kρ5/3,　P = ℛρT,　ℛ ≡ kBμmp
K ∝ GM1/3R
The last scaling follows by eliminating central density from the n = 3/2 Lane–Emden structure. Hold composition fixed. Partial ionization and a superadiabatic surface layer limit this idealized adiabat.

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior — 02Supply a photospheric pressure [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/hayashi/
Coverage: reading

In a thin plane atmosphere dP/dτ = g/κ. Taking representative g and κ at optical depth about 2/3 gives Pph ≈ 2g/(3κph). Supply an approximate low-temperature H⁻ opacity per unit mass; microscopic opacity theory is outside this chapter.
Pph ≈ 2g3κph,　g = GMR²
κ = κ₀ρaTb,　a = 12,　b = 7.7
The opacity power law has a limited temperature/composition range, and κ₀ carries units. Approximate the photospheric temperature by effective temperature to connect the atmosphere and interior; do not extrapolate the law to all stars.

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior — 03A strong opacity power gives a weak temperature scaling [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/hayashi/
Coverage: reading

Eliminate density with ρ = P/(ℛT). The adiabat then gives P = (ℛT)⁵ᐟ²K⁻³ᐟ². Substitute into the photospheric condition and collect the powers of K, M and R.
Pa+1 ∝ gκ₀−1ℛaTa−b
Teff3a+2b+5 ∝ κ₀−2Ma+3R3a−1Teff ∝ M3.5/21.9R0.5/21.9,　ε ≡ 143.8 ≈ 0.02283
L = 4πR²σSBTeff⁴ ∝ R2+4ε　(fixed M)
A large radius change produces little temperature change, giving a nearly vertical H–R path. The cool boundary is the Hayashi limit. This scaling estimates its slope; it does not replace a detailed atmosphere calculation or establish every forbidden configuration.

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior — 04Use luminosity to time contraction [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/hayashi/
Coverage: reading

Omit nuclear burning, accretion and mass loss, and take total energy E = −3GM²/(7R). Integrating L = −dE/dt gives a contraction law. Write ℓ = 2+4ε and use initial radius R₀ and luminosity L₀.
L = −3GM²7R²dRdt,　RR₀ = (1+tt₀)−1/(ℓ+1)
t₀ = 3GM²7(ℓ+1)R₀L₀

## 01A cool photosphere and convective interior — 05Leaving a Hayashi track [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/hayashi/
Coverage: reading

A radiative interior invalidates a single n = 3/2 convective model. Following a hotter Henyey track requires radiative transport and internal structure. Different nearly vertical curves need not describe the same mass or evolutionary phase.
Playback advances the specified energy model. It is more than moving an observational point, but it does not reproduce published numerical tracks. Separate the weak temperature dependence from the energy budget that sets elapsed time.

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNFormulate similarity collapse and distinguish Shu/LPH boundary conditions, sonic points and asymptotic profiles.

## 01The same equations can select different flows — 01The same equations can select different flows [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-collapse/
Coverage: reading

Begin with continuity, momentum and mass for a spherical self-gravitating isothermal gas. Cooling maintaining isothermality is supplied; rotation and fields are omitted. Enclosed M includes any central point mass.
∂ρ∂t+1r²∂∂r(r²ρu)=0
∂u∂t+u∂u∂r=−cs²∂lnρ∂r−GMr²,　∂M∂r=4πr²ρ
Shu and Larson–Penston are not automatically successive stages of one cloud. The sign of time relative to central formation and the outer density/velocity select the solution.

## 01The same equations can select different flows — 02Introduce post-formation similarity variables [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-collapse/
Coverage: reading

Set central formation at t = 0 and use t > 0 here. Match time powers in each term to reduce partial differential equations to ordinary equations in x.
x=rcst,　u=csv(x),　ρ=α(x)4πGt²,　M=cs³tGm(x)
m=x²α(x−v),　m′=x²α[(x−v)²−1]v′=[α(x−v)−2x](x−v)
[(x−v)²−1]α′α=[α−2(x−v)x](x−v)
A regular sonic point x−v = 1 also requires α = 2/x. Directly dividing by the vanishing denominator fails; select a regular critical branch together with global boundary conditions.

## 01The same equations can select different flows — 03Shu: inside-out collapse from a singular isothermal sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-collapse/
Coverage: reading

x≥1 :　v=0,　α=2x²,　ρ=cs²2πGr²
x→0 :　m→m₀,　v≈−√(2m₀x),　α≈√(m₀2x³)
The exterior is a static singular isothermal sphere, with an expansion wave at x = 1 marking the onset of infall. Inner r⁻³ᐟ² density and free-fall velocity apply where central mass dominates. Use the supplied global-solution result m₀ ≈ 0.975.
Ṁ=m₀cs³G,　Mcentral=m₀cs³tG

## 01The same equations can select different flows — 04LPH: an envelope already flowing inward [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-collapse/
Coverage: reading

The Larson–Penston flow continued through central formation by Hunter has α ≈ 8.86/x² and v ≈ −3.28 far away. The envelope is about 4.43 times denser than Shu’s static sphere and is already infalling. Its central coefficient is m₀ ≈ 46.9.

## 01The same equations can select different flows — 05Separate the derived equations from supplied eigenvalues [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-collapse/
Coverage: reading

The similarity equations and free-fall powers can be followed here. Coefficients 0.975 and 46.9 depend on boundary conditions and critical branches, not dimensions alone. The plots integrate the intermediate region from Shu’s A → 2⁺ limit and the LPH outer data, measuring the inner m₀. Checks use Shu (1977), Table 2, free-fall limits, mass conservation and half-step convergence. Rounded LPH outer coefficients give m₀ ≈ 46.97.
Finite reservoirs, disks, fields and heating change real accretion. Compare with collapse from a finite initial equilibrium such as a Bonnor–Ebert sphere, and inspect what was imposed at infinity.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — Princeton University tribute ↗ (14 April 2025). The article describes his scientific contributions and shares recollections from colleagues and former students.
Approximate a long molecular filament by a cylinder uniform along its axis. Gravity still balances pressure, as in a star, but changing geometry alters the density envelope and mass limit. Here the tribute takes the form of deriving and using a result, rather than only remembering its name.
We follow §III of the original paper. Ostriker’s footnote acknowledges the same solution obtained by Stodółkiewicz (1963); it is also called the Stodółkiewicz–Ostriker solution. Notation and length scales below are chosen for this guide.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 02From spherical mass to cylindrical line mass [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

Let r measure distance from the axis, and λ(r) be the enclosed mass per unit length. Line mass is mass divided by length, distinct from volume density or projected column density. Assume a long, static, isothermal, nonrotating cylinder, neglecting ends, magnetic fields and turbulent support.
Divide the mass in a segment by its length L. Gravity points toward the axis, perpendicular to it.λ(r)=∫0r2πr′ρ(r′)dr′,　dλdr=2πrρ
gr=−2Gλ(r)r
A cylindrical Gaussian surface has side area 2πrL, so gravity times 2πrL equals 4πGλL. This differs from spherical GM/r². Near a regular centre λ ∝ r², so gravity tends to zero linearly rather than diverging.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 03Reduce isothermal balance to one equation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

P=cs²ρ,　cs²dlnρdr=−2Gλr
1rddr(rdlnρdr)=−4πGρcs²
Write ρ = ρce−ψ, H = cₛ/√(4πGρc), and ξ = r/H. Regular central conditions are ψ(0) = ψ′(0) = 0. The spherical isothermal Lane–Emden term 2ψ′/ξ becomes ψ′/ξ for a cylinder.
ψ″+1ξψ′=e−ψ
This is not the spherical solution with a relabelled radius. Geometry changes the growth of Gaussian surface area, leaving a different coefficient in the differential equation.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 04Verify the analytic solution and its r⁻⁴ envelope [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

ψ=2ln(1+ξ²8),　ρ(r)=ρc(1+r²8H²)−2
a≡√8H,　ρ(r)=ρc(1+r²/a²)²ψ′=4ξ8+ξ²,　ψ″+ψ′ξ=64(8+ξ²)²=(1+ξ²8)−2
Density is nearly constant for r ≪ a and falls as r⁻⁴ for r ≫ a, unlike the r⁻² singular isothermal sphere. The scale a marks a smooth transition rather than an abrupt density boundary.
Unlike a profile known only by numerical integration, this closed form can be substituted to verify the equation identically. This also makes it a useful numerical benchmark.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 05Infinite radial extent but finite line mass [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

λ(r)=2πρc∫0rr′dr′(1+r′²/a²)²=πρca²r²r²+a²
λ(r)=2cs²Gr²r²+a²,　λcrit≡λ(∞)=2cs²G
Increasing central density narrows the cylinder, cancelling ρc from its full line mass. Radius a encloses half that line mass. The total mass of an infinitely long cylinder is still infinite: the finite quantity is mass per unit length.
cs²=kBTμmp,　λcrit≈16.5(T10 K)(2.33μ) M☉ pc−1
This static cylinder supported only by thermal pressure cannot support a larger line mass. Axial fragmentation, end-driven contraction and magnetic, rotational or turbulent support are separate questions; this one formula does not determine every real filament’s fate.

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 06Pressure truncation and observational projection [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/
Coverage: reading

Truncating at radius R with external pressure gives a line mass below the limit. Set f = λ(R)/λcrit to obtain the surface-to-central density relation algebraically.
R=a√(f1−f),　ρ(R)ρc=(1−f)²,　Pext=cs²ρ(R)
The side-on projected column density Σ integrates volume density along the line of sight. For the radially unbounded analytic model, it follows the formula below at projected distance b and falls as b⁻³, not r⁻⁴. A pressure-truncated cylinder has a different, finite line-of-sight integration range.
Σ(b)=∫−∞∞ρ(√(b²+z²))dz=πρca2(1+b²a²)−3/2

## 01In memory of Jeremiah P. Ostriker — 06Pressure truncation and observational projection [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/isothermal-cylinder/

## 01Two responses compete in a compression [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNLinearize continuity, momentum, and Poisson equations, derive the Jeans length and growth time, and explain the limits of subtracting background gravity.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Two responses compete in a compression — 01Two responses compete in a compression [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

A small overdensity attracts additional gas, while its pressure increase tends to erase the contrast. Examine individual wavelengths to determine where gravity wins. Exclude rotation, magnetic fields, viscosity, and expansion of the background in this model.
ρ = ρ₀(1+δ),　P = P₀+cs²ρ₀δ
cs² = (∂P∂ρ)background
The density contrast δ is dimensionless and satisfies |δ| ≪ 1. Isothermal and adiabatic responses have different sound speeds. Use the constant cs appropriate to the chosen closure rather than silently switching between them.

## 01Two responses compete in a compression — 02What happened to background gravity? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

An infinite uniform density at rest creates a background problem: its pressure gradient vanishes, but ordinary Poisson gravity requires ∇²Φ₀ = 4πGρ₀, not zero. A static homogeneous background with zero gravitational field is not self-consistent without an additional prescription.
To solve a finite cloud with its boundary conditions, use a different model such as a Bonnor–Ebert sphere. The purpose here is the local competition between pressure and gravity at each wavelength.

## 01Two responses compete in a compression — 03Eliminate velocity from three equations [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

Let u denote perturbation velocity. Linearize continuity and momentum, discarding quadratic terms such as δu. Differentiate continuity in time and substitute the divergence of momentum to obtain an equation for density contrast alone.
∂δ∂t+∇·u = 0
∂u∂t = −cs²∇δ−∇φ,　∇²φ = 4πGρ₀δ∂²δ∂t² = cs²∇²δ+4πGρ₀δ
The gravitational term reinforces a density peak. The spatial second derivative of a wave is negative at that peak, so the sound-speed term restores it. Checking these derivative signs explains the competition without memorizing the final criterion.

## 01Two responses compete in a compression — 04Replace derivatives with wavenumber and frequency [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

Insert a plane wave δ = δ₁exp[i(k·r−ωt)]. The second time derivative supplies −ω² and the Laplacian supplies −k². Positive ω² gives oscillations; negative ω² allows growing and decaying solutions with ω = iσ.
ω² = cs²k²−4πGρ₀
kJ = √(4πGρ₀)cs,　λJ = 2πkJ = cs√(πGρ₀)
Wavelengths longer than λJ are unstable. At short wavelengths the pressure term increases as k². The exact k = 0 mode changes the mean density and is excluded from the background-subtracted perturbations; the plotted long-wave endpoint is a formal limit.

## 01Two responses compete in a compression — 05Define length, mass, and time separately [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/jeans/
Coverage: reading

MJ ≡ 4π3ρ₀(λJ2)³ = π5/26cs³G3/2ρ₀1/2
tJ ≡ 1√(4πGρ₀),　σtJ = √[1−(k/kJ)²]
The numerical coefficient in a Jeans mass depends on the geometry and length used to convert a wavelength into mass. Here the sphere’s radius is half a wavelength. The wavelength criterion itself follows directly from the dispersion relation.
When δ approaches order unity, the omitted nonlinear terms matter. A linear growth rate does not predict the number or masses of the eventual stars. For a rotating thin disk, include the rotational response as in Toomre Q.

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNInclude advection in interface conditions, derive KH growth and pattern speed, and compare gravitational stabilization with shear driving.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

Two fluids moving at different speeds along an interface can transfer bulk kinetic energy into interface waves. This is Kelvin–Helmholtz instability. RT uses density ordering relative to effective acceleration; KH is driven by shear.
Use the RT chapter’s incompressible, inviscid, infinitely deep half-spaces, upper density ρ₁ and lower density ρ₂. Background velocities U₁ and U₂ are uniform and parallel to x, discontinuous at the interface. Initially set g = 0, with no surface tension or magnetic field and k > 0 parallel to the flow.
Rewrite the RT chapter’s potential flow and pressure conditions to include advection.

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 02Use the frequency seen by each stream [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

Following each stream changes the time derivative to ∂/∂t + Uj∂/∂x. Acting on ei(kx−ωt) gives −i(ω−kUj). Define this stream-relative frequency as Ωj.
Ω₁ = ω−kU₁,　Ω₂ = ω−kU₂
a₁ = iΩ₁ζ₀k,　a₂ = −iΩ₂ζ₀k
Pressure perturbations also use δpj = −ρj(∂/∂t + Uj∂/∂x)φj. Matching interface pressures gives, for g = 0:
ρ₁(ω−kU₁)² + ρ₂(ω−kU₂)² = 0

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 03Complete the square to separate drift and growth [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

Subtract the density-weighted mean velocity Ū and complete the square. With ΔU = U₁ − U₂:
Ū ≡ ρ₁U₁+ρ₂U₂ρ₁+ρ₂
(ω−kŪ)² = −ρ₁ρ₂(ρ₁+ρ₂)²k²(ΔU)²ω = kŪ ± iσ,　σ = √(ρ₁ρ₂)ρ₁+ρ₂k|ΔU|
The pattern drifts at Ū while one branch grows as eσt. Equal densities still give σ = k|ΔU|/2. Adding the same velocity to both streams changes drift but not growth. Larger density contrast reduces growth at the same shear.

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 04Combine gravity and shear in one relation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

Restore the RT chapter’s downward g. Pressure matching becomes ρ₁Ω₁² + ρ₂Ω₂² = (ρ₂−ρ₁)gk. Completing the square again puts gravity and shear in a single relation.
(ω−kŪ)² = −Agk − ρ₁ρ₂(ρ₁+ρ₂)²k²(ΔU)²
A ≡ ρ₁−ρ₂ρ₁+ρ₂
A positive right-hand side gives advected oscillations; a negative value gives growing and decaying branches. For A > 0, RT and KH both destabilize. For A < 0, gravity supports long waves, but the shear term proportional to k² wins at sufficiently short wavelengths.

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 05A linear wave is not a vortex simulation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/
Coverage: reading

Wave roll-up, vortices, and mixing are nonlinear stages. Jets and surrounding gas or gas clouds moving at different speeds are applications, but their stability depends on actual density, sound speed, magnetic field, and velocity profiles.
The panel draws a single linear mode of the derived dispersion relation, not a nonlinear fluid simulation. Comparing RT, KH, Jeans, and Toomre instabilities asks the same useful questions: what supplies energy, and what opposes the disturbance?

## 01Shear can drive waves even at equal density — 05A linear wave is not a vortex simulation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kelvin-helmholtz/

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kerr/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDistinguish the horizon from the static limit and compute the spin dependence of circular orbits.
Connect to foundations: An entry to general relativity: light, clocks, and gravity ↗

## 01A ruler for rotating spacetime — 01A ruler for rotating spacetime [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kerr/
Coverage: reading

Take the stationary, axisymmetric, asymptotically flat Kerr vacuum metric as a supplied exact solution. Set electric charge to zero and neglect disc and test-particle self-gravity. Use G = c = M = 1: restore physical lengths with GM/c² and times with GM/c³. Let χ = cJ/(GM²) be nonnegative and label the orbital direction separately.
Σ = R² + χ² cos²θ,　Δ = R² − 2R + χ²
ds² = −(1−2R/Σ)dt² − (4χR sin²θ/Σ)dt dφ + (Σ/Δ)dR² + Σdθ²
　+ [R²+χ²+2χ²R sin²θ/Σ]sin²θ dφ²
The mixed coefficient is 2gₜφ. Setting χ = 0 recovers Schwarzschild. R is a Boyer–Lindquist coordinate, not an areal radius when the spin is nonzero. We derive consequences of the metric; its construction from the Einstein equations is left to the references.

## 01A ruler for rotating spacetime — 02Separate the horizon and static limit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kerr/
Coverage: reading

The outer horizon is the larger root of Δ = 0. An observer at fixed R, θ, and φ has ds² = gₜₜdt² and can be timelike only where gₜₜ < 0. The outer root of gₜₜ = 0 defines the static limit.
R₊ = 1 + √(1−χ²)
Rₑ(θ) = 1 + √(1−χ²cos²θ)
The surfaces meet at the poles; the equatorial static limit remains at R = 2. Between them static observers are impossible, but rotating timelike observers can exist. The static limit is not a no-escape boundary. The panel stays in the exterior and does not evolve horizon-crossing or exactly extremal orbits.

## 01A ruler for rotating spacetime — 03Allowed angular velocities and frame dragging [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kerr/
Coverage: reading

Hold R and θ fixed and rotate at Ω = dφ/dt. Substitution into the metric gives a quadratic that must be negative. Its null endpoints bound the allowed angular velocities of stationary timelike observers.
gₜₜ + 2gₜφΩ + gφφΩ² < 0
Ω₋ < Ω < Ω₊,　Ω± = [−gₜφ ± √(gₜφ²−gₜₜgφφ)]/gφφ
ΩZ = −gₜφ/gφφ = 2χR / [(R²+χ²)²−χ²Δsin²θ]
Even zero angular momentum pφ = gₜφuᵗ + gφφuφ generally requires a nonzero ΩZ. This describes frame dragging of a locally nonrotating observer. Such a fixed-radius observer generally needs acceleration; ΩZ is not the angular velocity of a freely orbiting particle.

## 01A ruler for rotating spacetime — 04From circular orbits to the ISCO [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/kerr/
Coverage: reading

For a massive equatorial test particle, conserved specific energy E and angular momentum L separate the radial motion. The Carter constant vanishes in the equatorial plane. Combine the two circular-orbit conditions with marginal radial stability.
Σ²(dR/dτ)² = ℛ(R)
ℛ = [E(R²+χ²)−χL]² − Δ[R²+(L−χE)²]
ℛ = ∂Rℛ = ∂²Rℛ = 0
⇒ R² − 6R + 8sχ√R − 3χ² = 0
Differentiate at fixed E and L, then eliminate them. Set s = +1 for prograde and s = −1 for retrograde motion. Selecting the physical exterior branch yields the closed expression below.
Z₁ = 1 + (1−χ²)1/3[(1+χ)1/3+(1−χ)1/3]
Z₂ = √(3χ²+Z₁²)
RISCO,s = 3+Z₂−s√[(3−Z₁)(3+Z₁+2Z₂)]
At χ = 0 both branches give six. As χ approaches one, the prograde limit is one and the retrograde limit is nine. This does not place an ordinary timelike orbit on the exactly extremal horizon. Infall can occur inside the ISCO; pressure and magnetic stresses make a disc inner edge a different question.

## 01From global balance to a thin shell [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNNondimensionalize hydrostatic balance, integrate from centre to surface, and explain why mass is independent of central density for n = 3.
Connect to foundations: The Chandrasekhar mass: a white dwarf’s limit ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 01From global balance to a thin shell [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

A global estimate P ∼ GM²/R⁴ treats a star as a whole. Now distinguish the enclosed mass m(r), local pressure P(r), and density ρ(r). Assume a static, spherical, nonrotating object with Newtonian gravity.
A shell of thickness Δr has mass about 4πr²ρΔr. Its net outward pressure force is −4πr²ΔP; the inward gravitational force is (Gm/r²)4πr²ρΔr. Balance them and take the thin-shell limit.
dPdr = −Gmρr²
dmdr = 4πr²ρ

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 02Eliminate mass and close the model [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

Multiply the first equation by r²/ρ to get r²P′/ρ = −Gm. Differentiate with respect to r and use the second equation; here a prime means an r derivative.
1r²ddr (r²ρdPdr) = −4πGρ
We still need a pressure–density relation. Adopt a polytropic relation with K and n constant throughout the object. The index n is not a particle count: it specifies the density dependence of pressure.
P = Kρ1+1/n　(n > 0)

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 03Remove the units, step by step [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

Let ρc be the central density and use θ(ξ) to describe the profile. The scale a is a length; ξ and θ are dimensionless. Use d/dr = (1/a)d/dξ.
ρ = ρcθn,　r = aξ
P = Kρc1+1/nθn+11ρdPdr = (n+1)Kρc1/nadθdξ
Substitute into the previous equation. Transforming the outer 1/r² and derivative leaves a factor (n+1)Kρc1/n/a² on the left and 4πGρc on the right. Choose a so that these factors match.
a² = (n+1)K4πGρc(1−n)/n1ξ²ddξ(ξ²dθdξ) = −θn
θ″ + 2ξθ′ + θn = 0The Lane–Emden equation; primes now denote ξ derivatives.

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 04Start at a regular centre [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

By definition θ(0) = 1. A smooth spherical centre has no preferred direction, so θ′(0) = 0. Direct numerical evaluation at ξ = 0 would nevertheless give 0/0 in 2θ′/ξ.
Insert θ = 1 + Aξ² + Bξ⁴ + … . Constant terms give 6A + 1 = 0; ξ² terms give 20B + nA = 0. This supplies starting values just outside the centre.
θ = 1 − ξ²6 + nξ⁴120 + …
θ′ = −ξ3 + nξ³30 + …
Start at a small ε > 0 and integrate outward using two first-order equations, θ′ = q and q′ = −2q/ξ − θⁿ. The first zero ξ₁ defines the surface. Here we consider 0 < n < 5.

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 05Integrate and compare with a known solution [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

For n = 1, θ = sinξ/ξ is a solution. Differentiating ξ²θ′ = ξcosξ − sinξ gives −ξsinξ = −ξ²θ, as required. The central limit is one and the surface is ξ₁ = π. This provides an independent check on the numerical method.
The numerical recipe: fourth-order Runge–Kutta
For y′ = F(x,y), y can be a scalar or a vector such as (θ,q). With step h, calculate these four slopes in order.
k₁ = F(x,y)
k₂ = F(x+h/2, y+hk₁/2)
k₃ = F(x+h/2, y+hk₂/2)
k₄ = F(x+h, y+hk₃)
y(x+h) ≈ y(x) + h6(k₁+2k₂+2k₃+k₄)

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 06Turn the dimensionless shape into radius and mass [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

Integrating the equation from centre to surface expresses the density integral through the surface slope. Call this dimensionless mass μₙ.
μn ≡ ∫0ξ₁ ξ²θndξ = −ξ₁²θ′(ξ₁)
R = aξ₁,　M = 4πa³ρcμnR ∝ K1/2ρc(1−n)/(2n)
M ∝ K3/2ρc(3−n)/(2n)Scaling along a sequence at fixed n; G is also constant.
For n = 3/2, M ∝ ρc1/2 and R ∝ ρc−1/6, giving R ∝ M−1/3 at fixed K. For n = 3, the density exponent in M vanishes: changing radius does not change the equilibrium mass at the same K.

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 07Recover the Chandrasekhar coefficient [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/
Coverage: reading

In the cold, completely degenerate, ultrarelativistic electron limit, P = KURρ4/3, so n = 3. Quantum statistics supplies KUR below. Here μe is mass per electron in units of the atomic mass constant mu, and ℏ = h/(2π).
KUR = ℏc4(3π²)1/3(μemu)−4/3
Numerical integration gives ξ₁ ≈ 6.89685 and μ₃ ≈ 2.01824. Cancel the central density in a³ρc to recover the structural coefficient supplied in the foundation lesson.
M = 4πμ₃(KURπG)3/2
MCh ≈ 5.83μe² M☉ ≈ 1.46 M☉　(μe = 2)

## 01From global balance to a thin shell — 07Recover the Chandrasekhar coefficient [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lane-emden/

## 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lin-mestel-shu/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNCompute axis evolution and compare spherical, oblate, and prolate collapse.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
