## 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall — 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lin-mestel-shu/
Coverage: reading

Shape changes the directional gravitational field even at fixed initial density. Consider an isolated, homogeneous, nonrotating pressure-free spheroid: no expansion, magnetic field, turbulence, or shocks. Follow it only before an axis collapses. A spheroid is a shape; it does not imply that the cloud rotates.
a₁ = a₂ = a(t),　a₃ = c(t),　q = c/a
M = (4π/3)ρa²c = constant
ρ(t)/ρ₀ = a₀²c₀/[a(t)²c(t)]
Use the homologous velocity field vᵢ = (ȧᵢ/aᵢ)xᵢ. The interior gravitational acceleration of a homogeneous ellipsoid is also linear in xᵢ, so this gives a self-consistent homogeneous evolution before caustics. It is not an exact description of arbitrary nonuniform clouds.

## 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall — 02From gravitational coefficients to axis motion [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lin-mestel-shu/
Coverage: reading

Use the coefficients Aᵢ from the ellipsoidal-potential chapter. Inside the cloud gᵢ = −2πGρAᵢxᵢ and their sum is two. Applying the pressure-free equation of motion to surface elements produces two ordinary differential equations.
Aᵢ = a₁a₂a₃∫₀∞du/[(aᵢ²+u)√((a₁²+u)(a₂²+u)(a₃²+u))]
2Aₐ+Ac = 2
ä = −2πGρAₐa,　c̈ = −2πGρAcc
For oblate q < 1 use e = √(1−q²); for prolate q > 1 use e = √(1−q⁻²). Use series near the sphere to avoid cancellation. The acceleration includes the axis length as well as its gravitational coefficient.
q < 1:　Ac = 2[1−q arccos(q)/e]/e²
q > 1:　Ac = (1−e²)[ln((1+e)/(1−e))−2e]/e³
q = 1:　Aₐ = Ac = 2/3,　Aₐ = (2−Ac)/2

## 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall — 03Specify initial conditions and a time unit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lin-mestel-shu/
Coverage: reading

Use a₀ as the length unit and t₀ = (2πGρ₀)⁻¹/² as the time unit. The dimensionless initial conditions are a = 1, c = q₀, with both velocities zero. Primes denote derivatives with respect to dimensionless time τ.
D = ρ/ρ₀ = q₀/(a²c)
a″ = −DAₐa,　c″ = −DAcc
Changing the initial density rescales physical time by ρ₀⁻¹/². Varying q₀ at fixed a₀ and initial density also changes the mass. The panel integrates this stated family; it does not allow arbitrary initial contraction velocities or external pressures.

## 01Let a slightly nonspherical cloud fall — 04Check against spherical collapse and energy conservation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/lin-mestel-shu/
Coverage: reading

For a sphere, a = c = b and b″ = −2/(3b²). The initially stationary solution has the parametric form below, providing an independent numerical benchmark.
b = (1+cosη)/2,　τ = (√3/4)(η+sinη)
τff = π√3/4,　tff = √[3π/(32Gρ₀)]
Total energy supplies a second check. The mean squared coordinate along an axis is aᵢ²/5, which determines the kinetic energy per unit mass. Combine it with the potential-energy integral to obtain a dimensionless invariant.
ℋ = (2a′²+c′²)/10 − (q₀/5)I(a,c)
I = ∫₀∞du/[(a²+u)√(c²+u)]
q < 1: I = 2 arccos(q)/(a√(1−q²))
q > 1: I = 2 arcosh(q)/(a√(q²−1))
Oblate clouds flatten further; prolate clouds become more elongated. A vanishing axis makes the density diverge and ends the smooth homogeneous flow. Stop when either the smallest initial-normalized axis or the current short/long axis ratio reaches 0.03. This is a numerical cutoff, not a measured singularity time.

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive the dispersion relation from local tension and Coriolis forces, find the unstable band and maximum growth, and distinguish linear growth from transport saturation.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

A Keplerian disk has angular velocity decreasing outward, but specific angular momentum j = r²Ω increasing outward. Even when hydrodynamic axisymmetric radial exchanges are stable, magnetic coupling changes the problem. This is the starting point of magnetorotational instability, MRI.
q ≡ −d lnΩd ln r,　κ² = 4Ω²+dΩ²d ln r = 2(2−q)Ω²
Keplerian rotation has q = 3/2. The epicyclic frequency κ describes the oscillation of a slightly radially displaced orbit. Restrict the model to 0 ≤ q < 2, separating MRI from hydrodynamic Rayleigh instability.

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 02Magnetic tension acts like a weak spring [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

Imagine two fluid elements connected by a field line. The faster inner element stretches the connection, so tension slows the inner element and accelerates the outer one. Losing angular momentum lets the inner element move inward, while the outer element moves outward. The separation can reinforce itself.
Quantify this using a local rotating frame, ideal MHD, incompressibility, a uniform vertical field B, and axisymmetric disturbances with vertical wavenumber k. Define the Alfvén speed and a tension frequency squared K.
vA = B√(μ₀ρ),　K = k²vA²

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 03Coupled displacement equations [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

Let ξx be radial displacement and ξy azimuthal displacement. The linear local equations contain Coriolis forces, the differential-rotation tidal term, and tension. Take these equations as the ideal-MHD input, then derive their eigenvalues explicitly.
ξ̈x−2Ωξ̇y = (2qΩ²−K)ξx
ξ̈y+2Ωξ̇x = −Kξy
Substitute ξ ∝ exp(st), replacing first and second derivatives by s and s². A nonzero displacement requires the determinant of the coefficient matrix to vanish.
(s²+K−2qΩ²)ξx−2Ωsξy = 0
2Ωsξx+(s²+K)ξy = 0s⁴+(κ²+2K)s²+K(K−2qΩ²) = 0

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 04Solve a quadratic in s squared [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

Set b = kvA/Ω, z = s²/Ω², and κ̂² = 2(2−q). The quartic in s is a quadratic in z. A positive larger root permits an exponentially growing solution with s > 0.
z²+(κ̂²+2b²)z+b²(b²−2q) = 0
z+ = −(κ̂²+2b²)+√(κ̂⁴+16b²)2
For 0 < q < 2, the interval 0 < b² < 2q makes the constant term negative, producing one positive and one negative root. There is no growth at exactly b = 0; sufficiently strong tension, b² ≥ 2q, also suppresses this mode.

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 05Find the fastest-growing wavelength [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

Differentiate z+ with respect to b² and set the derivative to zero: −1 + 4/√(κ̂⁴+16b²) = 0. Rearrangement gives the optimum tension and maximum growth rate.
bmax² = q(4−q)4,　smax = qΩ2
q = 32 :　bmax² = 1516,　smax = 3Ω4
For Keplerian rotation the e-folding time is 4/(3Ω), about 0.21 of an orbit 2π/Ω. This is the linear growth time of a small perturbation, not a prediction of unlimited exponential amplification.

## 01Connect a faster inner orbit to a slower outer orbit — 06MRI growth does not fix a saturated alpha [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/mri/
Coverage: reading

A real mode must fit within the disk thickness. Resistivity, ambipolar diffusion, and other nonideal effects alter the result when field–gas coupling is weak. The ideal unstable band alone cannot establish activity throughout a protoplanetary disk.
Linear eigenvalues do not determine nonlinear saturation or the mean transport stress. Obtaining the alpha used in an alpha disk requires additional calculations including field geometry, nonlinear interactions, and boundary conditions.

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNSelect the outflow branch of the equation shared with Bondi flow and separate sonic regularity, heating, and outer boundary conditions.
Connect to foundations: Why is the Sun’s surface about 6,000 degrees? ↗

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? — 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

In a hot atmosphere such as the solar corona, a pressure gradient pushes gas outward. Begin with Newtonian gravity from a mass M and an isothermal, spherical, steady gas. Magnetic fields, rotation, and electron–ion temperature differences are not yet included.
cs² = kBTμmp
Here μ is the mean mass per particle in proton-mass units; the example specifies μ = 0.6. This is the isothermal sound speed, so do not insert the heat-capacity ratio used in an adiabatic sound speed.

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? — 02Apply mass and momentum conservation to a wind [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

Now define u > 0 as the outward velocity. A steady wind has constant mass-loss rate 4πr²ρu. Eliminating the density gradient yields the same speed equation as in the Bondi chapter; the branch and boundary conditions distinguish the physical flows.
Ṁwind = 4πr²ρu
(u−cs²u)dudr = 2cs²r−GMr²
The right-hand side is negative inside rs and positive outside. On the subsonic branch the left-hand coefficient is also negative, so du/dr can be positive even inside the critical radius. Inward gravity does not forbid outward acceleration: include the pressure gradient in the force budget.

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? — 03The smooth accelerating sonic branch [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

rs = GM2cs²,　x = rrs,　𝓜 = ucs
𝓜²−ln(𝓜²) = 4ln x+4x−3
Connect a subsonic branch at x < 1 to a supersonic branch at x > 1. Expanding numerator and denominator together at the sonic point gives d𝓜/dx = +1, compared with −1 for Bondi’s inward speed. Choosing the wrong branch reverses the physical interpretation.
The plot compares the two critical curves. Each point is obtained by bisection of the integrated relation, avoiding a numerical division by zero at the sonic point.

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? — 04Why hydrostatic balance can be insufficient [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

The static isothermal equation can also be integrated. Given a base radius r₀ and density ρ₀, its density profile is:
ρ(r)ρ₀ = exp[GMcs²(1r−1r₀)]
Density tends to a finite nonzero value at infinity. Such a static atmosphere cannot match a sufficiently low-pressure exterior. A confined outer boundary can permit a subsonic breeze, so writing the differential equation alone does not uniquely demand the transonic wind.

## 01Can a hot atmosphere remain static? — 05Solar scales and the cost of isothermality [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/parker-wind/
Coverage: reading

For one solar mass, T = 106 K, and μ = 0.6, the isothermal sound speed is about 117 km/s and the sonic point about 6.9 solar radii. Raising temperature moves the sonic point inward as pressure becomes stronger relative to gravity.
A velocity profile still leaves density normalization unspecified, so a base density or equivalent boundary condition is needed for mass loss. Expansion requires heating to remain isothermal; this model does not solve an energy equation. At large radius 𝓜² ≈ 4 ln x, so it does not even predict a finite terminal speed.
Real solar wind is magnetized, with heating, conduction, and weak collisionality also important. Use Parker flow as a basic transonic outflow model, distinct from a magnetized disk wind extracting rotational angular momentum.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

Regions slightly denser than the cosmic mean can attract more matter. Press–Schechter theory combines fluctuation statistics with a collapse criterion to estimate halo abundances by mass. A halo here means a gravitationally bound dark-matter structure.
Define density contrast δ, initially with |δ| ≪ 1. Include cosmic expansion and pressureless matter growth, extending beyond the static gas in the Jeans chapter. The statistical variable is the linearly extrapolated contrast; the actual late nonlinear density field is not assumed to remain Gaussian.
δ ≡ ρ−ρ̄ρ̄,　δL(t) = D(t)δL(t₀),　D(t₀) = 1

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 02Choose a mass scale by smoothing [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

Averages over small and large regions need not agree. Smooth the field with a normalized window W and call its rms fluctuation σ(M,t). A real-space top-hat averages uniformly within a comoving radius R, which differs from the final halo radius.
M = 4π3ρ̄m,0R³,　σ²(M,t) ≡ ⟨δL,R²⟩
σ(M,t) = D(t)σ(M,t₀)
Here ρ̄m,0 is the mean matter density at the reference epoch, matching the comoving volume used for number densities. Linear smoothing of an initially Gaussian field gives a zero-mean Gaussian with variance σ².
p(δL|M,t) = 1√(2π)σexp(−δL²2σ²)
σ measures fluctuation strength at a mass scale, not the density of an individual halo. A larger σ places more of the distribution above a fixed threshold.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 03Spherical collapse gives a threshold of 1.686 [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

Model a uniform overdense region as a pressureless sphere. Before shell crossing, each shell encloses constant mass M and obeys R̈ = −GM/R². The growing-mode solution can be written parametrically:
R = A(1−cos θ),　t = B(θ−sin θ),　A³ = GMB²
The sphere turns around at θ = π and formally reaches R = 0 at θ = 2π. Divide its density 3M/(4πR³) by the background density 1/(6πGt²) to obtain the nonlinear contrast.
1+δNL = 92(θ−sin θ)²(1−cos θ)³
At small θ, θ−sinθ ≈ θ³/6 and δNL ≈ 3θ²/20. Match this to linear growth proportional to t2/3, then extrapolate:
δL(θ) = 35[34(θ−sin θ)]2/3
δc = δL(2π) = 35(3π2)2/3 ≈ 1.68647

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 04Relate a Gaussian tail to a mass fraction [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

Define peak height ν = δc/σ. A smaller σ makes the same threshold rarer. First compute the probability P of exceeding it at one smoothing scale by substituting u = δL/σ.
P = ∫ν∞e−u²/2√(2π)du = 12erfc(ν√2)
The Press–Schechter prescription assigns mass fraction F(>M) = 2P to halos above M. The complementary error function erfc is a name for this Gaussian-tail integral, not an additional physical law.
F(>M,t) = erfc(ν√2),　ν = δcσ(M,t)
For ν = 1, P ≈ 0.1587 and F ≈ 0.3173; at ν = 2, F ≈ 0.04550, and at ν = 3, F ≈ 0.002700. The Gaussian tail makes high-ν objects rare. The factor of two has not yet been derived; it requires the next section.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 05The factor of two and cloud-in-cloud accounting [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

A region below threshold on one scale can lie inside a larger collapsing region. Smaller regions inside a larger halo can also be counted as separate objects. A single-scale tail probability alone does not consistently assign matter to halos.
Even as σ → ∞, P approaches only 1/2. Doubling restores total-mass normalization, but symmetry between positive and negative contrasts alone does not solve the nested-region problem.
Excursion-set theory follows one position from large smoothing scales to small ones and assigns mass at the first threshold crossing. A Gaussian field with a sharp-k window and constant barrier has independent random-walk increments; a reflection argument yields the factor of two.
Extension: the first-crossing distributionS ≡ σ²,　ffirst(S) = δc√(2π)S3/2exp(−δc²2S)
F(>M) = ∫0S(M)ffirst(S)dS
We supply the reflection-principle result without solving a diffusion equation here. Real-space top-hat and Gaussian windows correlate the steps, so this simple derivation cannot be transferred unchanged. Distinguish the PS prescription, including its mass–window mapping, from the conditions of an exact first-crossing solution.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 06Convert mass fractions into number densities [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

Let n(M,t)dM be the number of halos per comoving volume in the interval [M,M+dM]. Minus the differential of F gives the mass fraction in this interval. Multiply by mean comoving matter density and divide by the mass per halo.
M n(M,t)dM = −ρ̄m,0∂F∂MdM
When ν increases with M, ∂F/∂ν = −√(2/π)exp(−ν²/2) and ∂ν/∂M = (ν/M)|∂lnσ/∂lnM|. Apply the chain rule:
n(M,t) = √(2π)ρ̄m,0M²ν|∂lnσ∂lnM|e−ν²/2
n has units of inverse comoving volume per unit mass. Number per natural-log mass is Mn(M), whereas mass fraction per natural-log mass is ψ = M²n(M)/ρ̄m,0. These are distinct vertical axes and give different curve shapes.
ψ(M,t) ≡ M²n(M,t)ρ̄m,0,　∫0∞ψ(M,t)dlnM = 1
This is the distribution of halos present at each epoch, not a formation rate per unit time. Merger histories require further ingredients such as conditional mass distributions.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 07Use a power-law spectrum to expose time scaling [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

The power spectrum describes fluctuation strength by wavenumber. For P(k) ∝ kn and a window W(kR), substitute q = kR to factor out the radius dependence. Spectral index n is distinct from the mass-function notation n(M).
σ²(R) = 12π²∫0∞k²P(k)|W(kR)|²dk ∝ R−(n+3)
σ(M,t) ∝ D(t)M−p,　p = n+36
For a real-space top-hat, a pure power law extending over all wavenumbers converges for −3 < n < 1. The panel stays within this idealized range; it does not compute a transfer function or calibrate σ₈ for precision cosmology.
σ(M,t) = δcD(t)(MM₀)−p,　ν = (M/M₀)pD(t)
σ(M*,t) = δc　⇒　M*M₀ = D1/p
M₀ is an arbitrary reference mass where ν = 1 at D = 1. M* also maximizes the mass-weighted logarithmic distribution ψ. For n = −2, p = 1/6 and M* ∝ D⁶. Scaling the horizontal axis by M* makes curves at fixed n overlap.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 08What the plots do and do not reproduce [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

These teaching plots recompute the equations above. To compare with Bond et al. (1991), equation (3.9), identify their A with S = σ², their fᵥ with δc, and |dlnA/dlnM| with 2p. This gives the same ψ = √(2/π)pνexp(−ν²/2). Equivalently, keep A = σ²(t₀) fixed and use barrier fᵥ = δc/D(t) to obtain the same ν.
Checks cover the analytic expression, standard Gaussian-tail values, mass normalization, and time scaling. These are not copies of paper figures, N-body data, or curves using a CDM transfer function. For example, Bond et al. Figure 5a uses a CDM spectrum, unlike this power-law model.
Distinguish recomputing an equation, reproducing a named figure, and comparing measured data. A citation alone does not make curves computed under different conditions identical.

## 01From small fluctuations to halo abundances — 09From dark halos to luminous galaxies [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/press-schechter/
Coverage: reading

PS theory bridges initial fluctuations and halo statistics. It does not specify gas cooling, star-formation efficiency, supernova or active-galactic-nucleus heating, or post-merger morphology. One halo cannot automatically be equated with one observed luminous galaxy.
Predictions depend on spherical-collapse assumptions, filtering, and nonspherical dynamics. Use this chapter to understand exponential rarity and hierarchical formation, with simulation-calibrated mass functions needed for more precise observational predictions.

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive the interface dispersion relation from kinematic and pressure conditions, estimate growth from Atwood number and wavelength, and identify the linear regime.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy — 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

Denser fluid below lighter fluid is a stable arrangement under gravity. If the denser fluid is above, a small downward deformation lets dense fluid sink while lighter fluid rises. Releasing potential energy amplifies the deformation: Rayleigh–Taylor instability.
Take z upward and acceleration −g with g > 0. The upper and lower half-spaces have constant densities ρ₁ and ρ₂. Both are infinitely deep, incompressible, and inviscid, with no surface tension, magnetic field, or background flow. Unlike Jeans instability, we prescribe g rather than calculate perturbed self-gravity.
ζ(x,t) = Re[ζ₀ei(kx−ωt)],　k = 2πλ > 0

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy — 02Build a flow that decays away from the interface [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

In each fluid take potential perturbations δu = ∇φ. Incompressibility, ∇·δu = 0, gives ∇²φ = 0. With x dependence eikx, the vertical equation is φ″ − k²φ = 0. Choose solutions that decay far from the interface.
φ₁ = a₁e−kzei(kx−ωt)　(z > 0)
φ₂ = a₂ekzei(kx−ωt)　(z < 0)
The interface moves with the fluid, so its vertical speed ∂ζ/∂t equals ∂φ/∂z on either side. At first order evaluate at z = 0. Removing the common exponential gives:
−iωζ₀ = −ka₁ = ka₂
a₁ = iωζ₀k,　a₂ = −iωζ₀k

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy — 03Keep the background pressure gradient [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

Spatial integration of linearized momentum gives δpj = −ρj∂φj/∂t = iρjωφj. Pressures match at the displaced interface z = ζ. The equilibrium gradient dp₀/dz = −ρg adds a term −ρgζ when evaluating there.
δp₁−ρ₁gζ = δp₂−ρ₂gζ
δp₁−δp₂ = (ρ₁−ρ₂)gζ
The amplitudes above give δp₁ = −ρ₁ω²ζ₀/k and δp₂ = +ρ₂ω²ζ₀/k. Subtract them and cancel nonzero ζ₀.
−(ρ₁+ρ₂)ω²k = (ρ₁−ρ₂)g
ω² = −Agk,　A ≡ ρ₁−ρ₂ρ₁+ρ₂
A is the Atwood number. Denser fluid on top gives A > 0 and ω = ±i√(Agk); the growing branch has σ = √(Agk). If A < 0, ω is real and the interface supports gravity waves in this model.
τgrow = 1σ = √(λ2πAg)　(A > 0)

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy — 04Explore density contrast and wavelength [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

Multiplying both densities by the same factor leaves A unchanged. Reducing wavelength by four increases k by four and growth rate by two. The doubling time is ln2/σ; 1/σ is the e-folding time. With A = 0 and no shear, this ideal interface has neither restoring force nor growth.

## 01Denser fluid above can lower its potential energy — 05Separate linear growth from nonlinear mixing [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/rayleigh-taylor/
Coverage: reading

This calculation describes only the small-amplitude stage. Large bubbles, dense spikes, and entrainment require nonlinear dynamics. At contacts in supernova remnants, examine density ordering relative to the effective acceleration in a frame following the decelerating flow.

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNIntegrate weak encounters over impact parameter to derive the Coulomb logarithm and relaxation scaling, distinct from crossing time.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters — 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

A star orbits in a cluster’s mean field and is weakly deflected by individual neighbours. Relaxation measures accumulated velocity changes becoming comparable to the original velocity. It is distinct from direct physical collisions.
Two-body refers to an encounter approximation, not a two-star model of the cluster. Assume equal masses, roughly isotropic velocities and many weak deflections.

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters — 02One deflection and its encounter rate [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

For a nearly straight passage with relative speed v and sufficiently large impact parameter b, integrate the transverse gravitational acceleration over the encounter.
Δv⊥≈2Gmbv,　dΓ=2πb n v db
n is stellar number density. Add squared, randomly directed kicks. The larger number of distant encounters compensates their smaller individual deflections, giving comparable contributions per logarithmic impact interval.
d⟨Δv²⟩dt≈8πG²m²nv∫bminbmaxdbb=8πG²m²nvlnΛ
Λ≡bmaxbmin,　trel≈v³8πG²m²n lnΛ

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters — 03Coulomb logarithms and cluster scaling [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

The analogous encounter integral gives the name Coulomb logarithm, although the force here is gravity. Estimate bmin near the large-angle scale Gm/v² and bmax near the system size; the integral has physical cutoffs.
v²∼GNmR,　tcross∼Rv,　trel∼NlnΛtcross
Combining the preceding impulse model with a uniform virial sphere gives a coefficient of 1/6. Distribution-averaged coefficients differ; do not mix this estimate with a precision convention.

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters — 04Compare half-mass relaxation times [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

Adopt the following conventional equal-mass half-mass relaxation estimate. The coefficient 0.138 and choice ln(0.4N) are supplied conventions, not exact consequences of the uniform-sphere calculation.
tdyn≡√(rh³GNm),　trh≈0.138Nln(0.4N)tdyn
The panel fixes m = 0.5 solar masses, so total mass grows with N. Dynamical time scales as N⁻¹ᐟ² and relaxation approximately as N¹ᐟ²/lnN. Always state which quantities are held fixed.

## 01Smooth gravity and discrete encounters — 05Toward segregation and distribution evolution [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/relaxation/
Coverage: reading

A mass spectrum introduces systematic energy exchange and a tendency for heavier objects to segregate inward. Complete equipartition need not follow. Binaries, strong encounters, tides and stellar evolution also affect long-term evolution.
Collecting small mean changes and variances into continuous coefficients leads to the Fokker–Planck equation. This panel recomputes timescale estimates, not an N-body trajectory of a particular cluster.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNRead clock rates from the metric, derive an effective potential from conserved quantities, and distinguish the horizon, photon sphere, and ISCO.
Connect to foundations: An entry to general relativity: light, clocks, and gravity ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 01Start from a spacetime ruler [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

A metric specifies how spacetime intervals are measured. Take the Schwarzschild vacuum metric as the supplied solution, then derive consequences using conserved quantities and algebra. The full tensor calculation solving the field equations is outside this lesson.
ds²=−A(r)c²dt²+A(r)−1dr²+r²(dθ²+sin²θ dφ²)
A(r)=1−2rgr,　rg≡GMc²,　R≡rrg
Here r is the areal radius: spheres have area 4πr². It need not equal radial proper distance. Coordinate time t is normalized to a distant static clock. Our gravitational radius is rg = GM/c²; the Schwarzschild radius is twice this value.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 02Static clocks and light received far away [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

For a clock at fixed spatial coordinates, ds² = −c²dτ². Substitute zero spatial increments into the metric to obtain its proper time τ relative to t. A local clock continues to run normally for an observer beside it.
dτdt=√(1−2/R),　1+z=νemν∞=1√(1−2/R)
The redshift formula assumes a static emitter and a static receiver at infinity. Moving emitters require additional kinematic effects. Remaining static outside the horizon requires support; a stationary clock cannot be placed on the horizon itself. The panel keeps R strictly greater than two.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 03Reduce radial motion using conserved quantities [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

Consider a massive test particle moving freely in the equatorial plane. Time-translation and rotational symmetries supply the following conserved quantities. Neglect the particle’s self-gravity and radiative backreaction. These orbit constants are separate from the mass parameter defining the spacetime.
ℰ=A(r)dtdτ,　ℓ=r²dφdτ,　λ≡ℓcrg,　j≡λ²
Divide the metric by dτ², set ds²/dτ² = −c², and substitute the constants. Rearranging isolates the radial kinetic term. This is an exact test-particle relation in the supplied metric, rather than an expansion in weak gravity.
−c²=−c²ℰ²A+1A(drdτ)²+ℓ²r²
1c²(drdτ)²+U(R)=ℰ²
U(R)=(1−2R)(1+jR²)=1−2R+jR²−2jR³
Radial motion requires ℰ² ≥ U. The additional −2j/R³ term changes the inner barrier. U is dimensionless and is compared with squared energy ℰ², not with ℰ. A potential plot alone is not an integration of a chosen trajectory.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 04Find circular orbits and test stability [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

For a circular orbit, radial velocity and the potential slope vanish. Differentiate at fixed j and clear denominators. The resulting quadratic exposes both circular branches and the parameter at which they merge.
dUdR=2R²−2jR³+6jR⁴=0
R²−jR+3j=0,　R±=j ± √(j²−12j)2
j=R²R−3　(R>3)
A minimum gives radial stability; a maximum gives instability. Evaluate the second derivative while keeping angular momentum fixed, and then insert the circular-orbit relation. Do not differentiate along a family that changes j.
d²UdR²circular=2(R−6)R³(R−3)
R>6: stable,　3<R<6: unstable
RISCO=6,　jISCO=12,　ℰISCO=√89
At R = 6 the radial restoring term vanishes: this is the marginal endpoint of the stable circular branch. ISCO does not forbid matter at smaller radii. Infall and accelerated fluid motion are different from stable circular test-particle geodesics.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 05The photon sphere and horizon are different radii [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

For light, use ds² = 0. The rest-mass term disappears and the normalized radial potential is W below. Differentiate it to locate the circular null orbit. Its maximum makes that orbit unstable.
W(R)=1−2/RR²,　dWdR=−2R³+6R⁴=0　⇒　Rph=3
The horizon is at R = 2, the photon sphere at R = 3, and the ISCO at R = 6. They are not three material surfaces. Also distinguish the photon orbit’s areal radius from the impact parameter for capture measured at infinity.
Sketch of areal-radius ratios; neither a proper-distance diagram nor an image of the distant shadow.bcrit=rph√A(rph)=3√3 rg
Schwarzschild-coordinate coefficients become singular at the horizon, whereas suitable coordinates describe infall across it. This differs from the curvature singularity at r = 0. The panel remains outside and does not draw horizon-crossing trajectories.

## 01Start from a spacetime ruler — 06Run clocks and compare circular branches [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/schwarzschild/
Coverage: reading

Clock playback advances distant time and computes the proper time of a supported clock at fixed radius. Changing angular momentum compares potential curves; it does not animate a test-particle orbit. The two operations answer different questions.
Try j = 16 to compare an unstable circular orbit at R = 4 with a stable one at R = 12. At j = 12 they merge at R = 6; below that value no massive circular geodesic exists. Use the displayed branch labels to connect the graph to the algebra.
The solution library compares this exact vacuum solution with fluid equilibria and similarity models. Kerr and Tomimatsu–Sato entries are listed with their lesson availability explicitly distinguished.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive the expansion law, reduce conservation laws to similarity ODEs, and use shock conditions and total energy to determine profiles and normalization.
Connect to foundations: Stellar structure and the mass–luminosity relation ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 01When does a blast wave become simple? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

Deposit energy E in a small region of a uniform gas of density ρ₀. A spherical blast expands into initially stationary material with negligible upstream pressure compared with the shock pressure. Use an ideal gas with constant heat-capacity ratio γ, neglecting gravity, magnetic forces, and radiative cooling.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 02Obtain the two-fifths exponent from units [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

Write the shock radius as R = C Eᵃρ₀ᵇtᶜ. Since [E] = ML²T⁻² and [ρ₀] = ML⁻³, matching mass, length, and time gives three linear equations for the exponents.
a+b = 0,　2a−3b = 1,　−2a+c = 0
a = 15,　b = −15,　c = 25R = β(Et²ρ₀)1/5,　D ≡ dRdt = 2R5t
Call the dimensionless coefficient β. The energy estimate E ∼ ρ₀R³D² gives the same exponents, but dimensional analysis cannot determine β. We must solve the interior and normalize its total energy.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 03Derive the Rankine–Hugoniot conditions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

In the shock frame, label upstream and downstream states 1 and 2. Let positive w₁ and w₂ be their speeds through the front. Across a thin, steady front, mass, momentum, and energy fluxes per unit area must match.
ρ₁w₁ = ρ₂w₂
p₁+ρ₁w₁² = p₂+ρ₂w₂²
w₁²2 + γp₁(γ−1)ρ₁ = w₂²2 + γp₂(γ−1)ρ₂
The last equation is energy flux divided by the common mass flux. Pressure work adds to internal energy to give the specific enthalpy γp/[(γ−1)ρ].
Set χ = ρ₂/ρ₁, so w₂ = w₁/χ. Momentum conservation gives p₂ = p₁ + ρ₁w₁²(1−1/χ). Substitute both into the energy equation and remove the unchanged-flow root χ = 1 to obtain the compressive solution.
χ = (γ+1)ℳ₁²(γ−1)ℳ₁²+2,　ℳ₁² ≡ ρ₁w₁²γp₁
For a strong shock, ℳ₁ ≫ 1, χ tends to (γ+1)/(γ−1). With stationary upstream gas, w₁ = D and the downstream laboratory speed is u₂ = D − w₂. A monatomic gas with γ = 5/3 gives χ = 4, u₂ = 3D/4, and p₂ = 3ρ₀D²/4.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 04Combine time and radius into one variable [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

Let u be outward fluid velocity, p pressure, and ρ density. In the smooth interior, spherical mass conservation, momentum balance, and adiabatic evolution hold. A partial derivative ∂ varies one coordinate while holding the other fixed.
∂ρ∂t + 1r²∂∂r(r²ρu) = 0∂u∂t + u∂u∂r = −1ρ∂p∂r(∂∂t+u∂∂r) (pργ) = 0
Set x = r/R and scale density by ρ₀, velocity by D, and pressure by ρ₀D². Seek profiles G, V, and Z depending only on x.
ρ = ρ₀G(x),　u = DV(x),　p = ρ₀D²Z(x)
∂x∂r = 1R,　∂x∂t = −DRx
For example, ∂ρ/∂t = −ρ₀(D/R)xG′, while the mass-flux derivative is ρ₀(D/R)(G′V + GV′ + 2GV/x). Cancelling the common factor gives the first equation below.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 05Similarity ODEs and shock boundaries [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

R ∝ t2/5 implies R(dD/dt)/D² = −3/2. Apply the same chain rule to momentum and adiabatic evolution to reduce the PDEs to three ODEs. Primes now denote x derivatives.
(V−x)G′ + G(V′+2V/x) = 0(V−x)V′ − 32V = −Z′G(V−x)(Z′Z−γG′G) = 3
The shock is at x = 1. Nondimensionalizing the jump conditions supplies the starting values.
G(1) = γ+1γ−1,　V(1) = 2γ+1,　Z(1) = 2γ+1
For γ = 5/3 integrate inward from (4, 3/4, 3/4). The physical branch approaches V → 0, G → 0, and finite Z at the centre. Low density can coexist with finite pressure, unlike a uniformly filled expanding sphere.

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 06Normalize the total energy to determine β [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

Total energy is kinetic plus internal energy. Their densities are ρu²/2 and p/(γ−1); integrate using the volume element 4πr²dr.
I ≡ ∫01 [GV²2+Zγ−1]x²dx
E = 4πρ₀R³D²I = 16π25ρ₀R⁵t²Iβ = (2516πI)1/5 ≈ 1.15167　(γ = 5/3)

## 01When does a blast wave become simple? — 07Use conservation laws to challenge the solution [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sedov/
Coverage: reading

The enclosed mass should equal the initially swept volume times density, 4πρ₀R³/3. Thus 3∫Gx²dx = 1 is an independent check. Refine the step and verify convergence of the mass ratio and β.
Radius and shock speed imply t = 2R/(5D). With density as well, infer E = ρ₀R⁵/(β⁵t²). Observed gas velocity is not generally the shock-front speed D.

## 01A shock can do more than heat gas [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNState the assumptions of diffusive shock acceleration, obtain the relativistic-particle energy slope from compression, and distinguish acceleration time from maximum energy.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01A shock can do more than heat gas — 01A shock can do more than heat gas [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/
Coverage: reading

Shock jump conditions constrain the bulk gas, but particles that repeatedly cross the front can form a high-energy population far from thermal equilibrium. Consider a steady, planar, nonrelativistic shock and particles with speed v ≈ c.
In the shock frame let w₁ > w₂ > 0, with compression χ = w₁/w₂. Scattering by magnetic irregularities approximately isotropizes particles in each local fluid frame. Use the test-particle limit, in which accelerated particles do not modify the flow.

## 01A shock can do more than heat gas — 02Gain and escape in one cycle [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/
Coverage: reading

For a small relative flow speed, changing between fluid frames changes relativistic particle energy by approximately (w₁−w₂)μ/v to first order. Crossing particles are flux-weighted by the direction cosine μ, giving mean μ = (∫₀¹μ²dμ)/(∫₀¹μdμ) = 2/3. Two crossings make a complete cycle.
g ≡ ⟨ΔEE⟩cycle ≈ 4(w₁−w₂)3v
For downstream particle number density np, the isotropic crossing flux is npv/4. The advective loss flux far downstream is npw₂. Their ratio gives the escape probability per cycle.
Pesc ≈ 4w₂v
Both are first order in flow speed divided by particle speed. This is the origin of the name first-order Fermi acceleration. This idealization neglects escape through the upstream boundary.

## 01A shock can do more than heat gas — 03Eliminate the cycle count to get a power law [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/
Coverage: reading

Start N₀ particles at E₀. After k surviving cycles, Ek = E₀(1+g)k and Nk = N₀(1−Pesc)k. Take logarithms and eliminate k.
N(>E)N₀ = (EE₀)ln(1−Pesc)/ln(1+g)
N(>E) ∝ E−Pesc/gThe second line uses ln(1+g) ≈ g and ln(1−Pesc) ≈ −Pesc for small gains and losses.
N(>E) is cumulative. The differential number distribution per energy interval is its negative derivative, adding one to the index.
dNdE ∝ E−s,　s = 1+Pescg
s = 1+3w₂w₁−w₂ = χ+2χ−1
A strong monatomic gas shock has χ = 4, giving s = 2 for relativistic particles per energy interval. A momentum-space distribution function uses dN = 4πp²f(p)dp, so the same solution is written f(p) ∝ p−4.

## 01A shock can do more than heat gas — 04A spectral slope does not set the maximum energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/
Coverage: reading

Describe scattering by spatial diffusion coefficients κ₁ and κ₂. A residence layer has thickness of order κ/w. Dividing its particle content by crossing flux nv/4 gives tcycle ≈ (4/v)(κ₁/w₁ + κ₂/w₂). Dividing by the gain g gives the local logarithmic acceleration time.
tacc(E) ≡ EdE/dt ≈ 3w₁−w₂(κ₁w₁+κ₂w₂)
For χ = 4 and κ₁ = κ₂ = κ, tacc = 20κ/w₁². If κ depends on energy, the time from E₀ to E is ∫tacc(E′)dE′/E′, not generally a single value of this local timescale.
rL = p|q|B ≲ L　⇒　E ≈ pc ≲ |q|BLcSI units; L is the size of the acceleration region.

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/shock-acceleration/

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive the equilibrium radius from photon and recombination balance, solve volume growth, and explain the neglect of gas motion.
Connect to foundations: Atomic light and starlight: why 10.2 eV? ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

Hot stars create regions of ionized hydrogen, called H II regions. Ionizing hydrogen requires a photon of at least 13.6 eV, unlike the 10.2 eV Lyα transition. The relevant input is the rate of photons above this threshold, rather than total stellar luminosity alone.
S ≡ ∫ν₀∞Lνhνdν,　hν₀ = 13.6 eV
S has units s−1: divide spectral luminosity by photon energy before integrating. Assume pure hydrogen of uniform nucleus density nH, a constant source S, no dust, and a nearly fully ionized interior.

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 02Count the recombinations that remove ions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

Recombination occurs at rate αnenp. Direct recombination to the ground state emits another ionizing photon. In the on-the-spot approximation, that photon is absorbed nearby, so this event does not require a new stellar photon in the net ionization budget.
αB(10⁴ K) ≈ 2.6 × 10−13 cm³ s−1
dNrecdt dV = αBnenp ≈ αBnH²
The Case B coefficient αB counts recombinations to excited states and is supplied at the assumed temperature. The same photon budget does not apply unchanged to a cloud that leaks ionizing photons or absorbs many of them in dust.

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 03Balance supply and consumption to find the radius [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

Let the ionized volume be V = 4πR³/3. In equilibrium, the supplied photon rate equals the integrated net recombination rate. At constant density, solving this volume balance gives the radius directly.
S = 4π3RS³αBnH²
RS = [3S4παBnH²]1/3
For S = 1049 s−1, nH = 100 cm−3, and the stated coefficient, RS is about 3.15 pc. Doubling density quadruples recombinations per unit volume and reduces the radius by 2−2/3.

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 04Excess photons ionize newly reached gas [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

Start with neutral gas. During growth, some photons replace recombinations in the existing ionized volume, while the remainder ionizes nHdV/dt newly reached atoms. The gas stays fixed; only the ionization boundary advances.
nHdVdt = S−αBnH²V
dVdt+Vtrec = SnH,　trec = 1αBnH
Set V = VS + W. Then W′ = −W/trec, so W decays exponentially. Apply V(0) = 0 and take the cube root only after obtaining the volume.
V(t) = VS[1−e−t/trec]
R(t) = RS[1−e−t/trec]1/3

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 05An ionization radius versus an expanding H II region [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

In the example, trec is about 1.22 × 10³ yr. At one recombination time, volume is about 63% and radius about 86% of equilibrium. Radius does not advance linearly with time. The circle marks an ionization boundary, not the velocity of all the gas.
In a clumpy medium, recombinations depend on the average of nenp, not simply the square of mean density. A measured radius alone does not uniquely determine stellar age or photon output without checking leakage, dust, density structure, and dynamical stage.

## 01Count ionizing photons, not just luminosity — 05An ionization radius versus an expanding H II region [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/stromgren/
Coverage: reading

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNCompute Compton y from scattering probability and electron temperature, derive the thermal SZ frequency dependence, and explain what the signal measures about cluster electron pressure.
Connect to foundations: The classical electron radius and Thomson scattering ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

The cosmic microwave background (CMB) has a nearly blackbody spectrum at 2.7255 K. Hot, diffuse gas fills the space between galaxies in a cluster. Its free electrons scatter passing CMB photons, which on average gain energy through inverse Compton scattering. The resulting small spectral distortion in the cluster direction is the thermal Sunyaev–Zel’dovich (SZ) effect.
Thermal SZ is driven by random electron motion. Kinetic SZ, due to the cluster’s bulk line-of-sight motion, is introduced separately in the final extension. This chapter focuses on the connection between cluster gas and observables, taking the scattering collision operator as a physical input.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 02Gain per scattering and scattering probability [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Let ε be photon energy and Te electron temperature. Use nonrelativistic electrons and the Thomson limit: photon energy in the electron rest frame is much smaller than mec². For isotropic photons and electrons, averaging to second order in electron speed gives fractional energy gain (4/3)⟨v²/c²⟩.
⟨v²⟩ = 3kBTeme
⟨Δεε⟩ ≈ 43⟨v²⟩c² = 4θe,　θe ≡ kBTemec²
For a uniform electron density ne and line-of-sight depth L, the scattering optical depth is τ = neσTL. The probability of at least one scattering is 1 − e−τ, approximately τ when τ ≪ 1.
τ = neσTL,　y ≡ τθe
Mean photon energy gainInitial energy ≈ 4yThe final line estimates the ensemble mean energy gain to first order, not the brightness change at every frequency.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 03y adds electron pressure along the line of sight [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

If density and temperature vary along the path, add contributions dy = θe neσT dl. Introducing ideal-gas electron pressure Pe = nekBTe makes the observable explicit.
y = ∫ kBTemec²neσTdl = σTmec²∫ Pedl
σT is an area and Pe dl has units of energy per area, so y is dimensionless. Pe is electron pressure, distinct from total gas pressure including ions. At constant temperature y/τ gives θe, but a thermal SZ signal alone does not separately determine τ and Te.
kBTe = 8 keV,　ne = 10−3 cm−3,　L = 1 Mpc
τ ≈ 2.05×10−3,　θe ≈ 8511,　y ≈ 3.21×10−5
A temperature quoted in keV denotes the thermal energy kBTe. Eight keV corresponds to about 9.3×10⁷ K. Use 1 cm⁻³ = 10⁶ m⁻³ and 1 Mpc ≈ 3.086×10²² m.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 04Calculate a distortion of the Planck spectrum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Write CMB temperature as T₀ and frequency as ν, and define x = hν/(kBT₀). Express the Planck spectrum using photon occupation N₀ and intensity scale I₀. N₀ is distinct from electron number density ne.
N₀(x) = 1ex−1,　Bν = I₀x³N₀(x)
I₀ = 2(kBT₀)³h²c²
For hot nonrelativistic electrons, τ ≪ 1, and y ≪ 1, take the following first-order consequence of the Kompaneets scattering equation as input. It represents repeated small energy changes as diffusion in frequency space.
ΔN ≈ yx²ddx(x⁴dN₀dx)
Insert dN₀/dx = −eˣ/(eˣ−1)². Apply the product rule to x⁴ and the exponential term, then use ΔIν = I₀x³ΔN. Collecting terms exposes the bracket that controls the sign.
ΔIν = I₀y x⁴ex(ex−1)² f(x)
f(x) ≡ xex+1ex−1−4

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 05The low-frequency deficit and the approximately 217 GHz null [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

For x ≪ 1, eˣ ≈ 1+x and x(eˣ+1)/(eˣ−1) tends to two. Thus f(x) tends to −2: a low-frequency deficit. Photons have gained energy and been redistributed toward higher frequencies.
Brightness differences are often expressed as the temperature change that would produce them in a blackbody. Linearize ΔIν = (∂Bν/∂T)ΔT and differentiate the Planck function:
∂Bν∂T = I₀T₀x⁴ex(ex−1)²
ΔTthT₀ = yf(x)　→　−2y　(x ≪ 1)
This is a frequency-dependent conversion to CMB thermodynamic temperature, not a claim that the distorted spectrum is a blackbody at a different single temperature. Solving f(x) = 0 gives x ≈ 3.830, or ν ≈ 217.5 GHz at T₀ = 2.7255 K. The thermal signal changes sign across this null.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 06What does this tell us about a cluster? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Measuring y across the sky gives a map of line-of-sight electron pressure. Combine it with other temperature and density measurements to study hot intracluster gas. A pressure-column measurement is not by itself a direct measurement of total cluster mass.
The first-order thermal spectrum cannot distinguish hotter, lower-density gas from cooler, denser gas with the same y. Multiple frequencies help isolate its sign-changing spectrum, while dust emission, radio sources, and primary CMB fluctuations must also be considered.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 07Extension: distinguish kinetic SZ [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Bulk motion of cluster gas relative to the CMB creates a Doppler signal distinct from random thermal motion. Take v_∥ positive away from the observer, with small optical depth and |v_∥| ≪ c.
ΔTkinT₀ ≈ −τv∥c
Receding gas gives a negative signal and approaching gas a positive one. At this order the thermodynamic-temperature shift is independent of frequency and has no thermal-style sign reversal near 217 GHz. The intensity difference still depends on frequency through ∂Bν/∂T.
Selecting a velocity in the panel adds kinetic SZ to the thermal intensity difference. For example, τ ≈ 0.00205 and v_∥ = 300 km/s give ΔTkin ≈ −5.60 μK. Kinetic SZ and other emission can remain at the thermal null, so 217 GHz does not isolate the unmodified background.

## 01Use background light to measure a galaxy cluster — 07Extension: distinguish kinetic SZ [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/sunyaev-zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNRelate magnetic field and electron energy to frequency, derive the spectrum from a power-law electron population, and explain how cooling alters the high-energy emission.
Connect to foundations: The classical electron radius and Thomson scattering ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

Besides thermal blackbody emission, nearly light-speed electrons radiate when bent by magnetic fields. This synchrotron emission can appear from radio to X-rays. Use SI units, with electron charge magnitude e and mass me.
γ = 1√(1−v²/c²),　E = γmec²
rL = γmev⊥eB
Here γ is the Lorentz factor, not the gas heat-capacity ratio used in the blast-wave chapter. For γ ≫ 1, total and kinetic energies are nearly equal. The pitch angle ψ is between velocity and magnetic field, with v_⊥ = v sinψ.

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 02Why does the frequency scale as γ²? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

Define the nonrelativistic gyrofrequency νB = eB/(2πme). A relativistic electron gyrates more slowly, but its radiation is beamed into an angle of order 1/γ. The observer sees only the short interval when the beam points toward them, further shortened by arrival-time compression.
For trajectory curvature radius ρcurv, the emission interval is of order ρcurv/(γc), and arrival-time compression adds another factor of order γ². The characteristic angular frequency is therefore of order γ³c/ρcurv. Using ρcurv ≈ γmec/(eB sinψ) gives γ²B sinψ. Radiation theory supplies the exact critical-frequency normalization below.
νc = 3eB4πmeγ² sinψ
νc ≈ 4.20 Hz × (B1 μG)γ² sinψ

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 03The Thomson cross section returns [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

Expressing relativistic radiative power through the Thomson cross section σT connects to the foundation lesson. Use magnetic energy density UB. For one pitch angle, P = 2σTcUBγ²(v/c)²sin²ψ; isotropic directions give ⟨sin²ψ⟩ = 2/3.
UB = B²2μ₀,　⟨P⟩ = 43σTcUBγ²　(γ ≫ 1)
This notation does not mean the electron is Thomson-scattering photons here. The same classical charge–mass combination is expressed using σT; the relativistic radiation formula is a physical input. μ₀ is the vacuum permeability.
tsyn ≡ E⟨P⟩ = 3mec4σTUBγ ∝ 1B²γ
At fixed B, higher-energy electrons cool faster. At a fixed observing frequency, γ ∝ (ν/B)1/2, so the cooling time scales as B−3/2ν−1/2.

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 04Convert the electron slope to the light spectrum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

Let the electron number distribution be N(E)dE = C E−sdE. Assume optically thin emission at fixed magnetic field, away from cooling breaks and energy cutoffs. For the derivation, concentrate each electron’s power near a representative frequency ν ∝ BE².
jνdν ∝ N(E)P(E)dE
jν ∝ E−s × B²E² × 1BE
jν ∝ B(s+1)/2ν−(s−1)/2
The Jacobian dν/dE ∝ BE is essential. Define the emissivity slope by jν ∝ ν−α; then α = (s−1)/2. The preceding chapter’s s = 2 gives α = 1/2. Integrating the full single-electron radiation kernel gives the same slope over a sufficiently broad power-law range.

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 05Solve the cooling equation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

At fixed magnetic field with ultrarelativistic, isotropically distributed electrons, dγ/dt = −aγ². Here a is a positive cooling coefficient used only in this chapter. Separate variables and integrate from the initial value γ₀.
a = 4σTUB3mec
1γ(t) − 1γ₀ = at
γ(t) = γ₀1+aγ₀t
For steady injection Q(E) ∝ E−s, the cooled high-energy steady distribution follows from number conservation in energy space. Neglect escape and let dot E = −bE²:
ddE[ĖN(E)] = Q(E),　Ė ≡ dEdt = −bE²
N(E) = 1bE²∫E∞Q(E′)dE′ ∝ E−(s+1)　(s > 1)
The electron index steepens by one, so α steepens by one-half. This cooling break assumes constant field, continuous injection, and radiative-loss dominance. An impulsive population or significant escape need not have the same spectrum.

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 06Return to observations with the assumptions intact [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/
Coverage: reading

Shock acceleration followed by radiation in a magnetic field produces a continuum that gas temperature alone cannot describe. Higher frequencies probe more energetic electrons, which also cool faster.

## 01A magnetic field bends the electron trajectory — 06Return to observations with the assumptions intact [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/synchrotron/

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive tensor virial balance, solve Maclaurin and Jacobi equilibria, and distinguish bifurcation from stability.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction — 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

Different directions can require different support even at the same total energy. Differentiate the inertia tensor twice and use continuity and Euler’s equation. For an unmagnetized fluid with zero surface pressure:
Iᵢⱼ = ∫ρxᵢxⱼdV,　Tᵢⱼ = 12∫ρvᵢvⱼdV
12Ïᵢⱼ = 2Tᵢⱼ+Wᵢⱼ+Πδᵢⱼ
Wᵢⱼ = −∫ρxᵢ∂Φ∂xⱼdV,　Π=∫PdV
Taking the trace recovers the scalar virial theorem. Random orbital stresses enter a stellar system; here Π represents fluid pressure. Do not double count these supports.

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction — 02Insert the homogeneous ellipsoid [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

Iᵢᵢ = Maᵢ²5,　Wᵢᵢ = −2πGρAᵢIᵢᵢ
Φeff = Φ−Ω²2(x²+y²)
A uniformly rotating incompressible fluid obeys P/ρ + Φeff = constant in its rotating frame. Zero pressure at the surface requires equal effective potentials at the three axis endpoints.
(A₁−ω²/2)a²=(A₂−ω²/2)b²=A₃c²,　ω² ≡ Ω²πGρ

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction — 03Maclaurin: an axisymmetric sequence [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

For a = b, q = c/a and e = √(1−q²), the two equatorial conditions coincide. Use the previous chapter’s coefficients to obtain angular speed.
ω²=2(A₁−q²A₃)
 = 2√(1−e²)e³[(3−2e²)sin−1e−3e√(1−e²)]
At small e, ω² ≈ (8/15)e², so the spherical limit has zero rotation. Angular speed does not grow monotonically forever with flattening; it need not uniquely identify a figure.

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction — 04Jacobi: solve a triaxial sequence [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

Set a = 1 and use p = b/a and q = c/a. Eliminate ω² between the equatorial conditions. The panel integrates Aᵢ and bisects for q at each specified p.
A₂−A₁p−2−1=A₃q²,　ω²=2(A₁−A₃q²)
As a reference check, Fitzpatrick Table 2.2 gives e₁₃ ≈ 0.85585 and Ω/√(2πGρ) ≈ 0.42827 at e₁₂ = 0.6, or b/a = 0.8. The panel uses Ω²/(πGρ); divide by two and take its square root before comparing to that table.
The two controls select independent equilibria, not an equal-angular-momentum pair. These are calculated equilibrium sequences, not a time-dependent transformation from one fluid shape to another.

## 01Track the second derivative of size along each direction — 05Bifurcation and two kinds of instability [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tensor-virial/
Coverage: reading

The Jacobi branch bifurcates near e = 0.81267 and T/|W| = 0.1375. Weak-viscosity secular instability differs from the ideal-fluid dynamical bar instability near e = 0.95289 and T/|W| = 0.2738. These thresholds are supplied perturbation results.
T=MΩ²(a²+b²)10,　W=−2πGρM5ΣAᵢaᵢ²

## 01The third atom carries away energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConserve hydrogen nuclei, solve the molecular fraction, and compare density-dependent chemical and gravitational clocks.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
Compare solution assumptions and boundary data in the solution library ↗

## 01The third atom carries away energy — 01The third atom carries away energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

H + H + H → H₂ + H
To form a bound molecule, two hydrogen atoms must transfer excess energy and momentum elsewhere. A third atom provides a collision partner that can carry them away. That partner remains an H atom after the event. This is a three-body chemical reaction, distinct from gravitational encounters involving three stars.
This offers an entry to molecular formation in dense primordial gas with little dust. Supply a reaction coefficient and focus on how rapidly composition changes. The microscopic potential and quantum-scattering calculation belong in the reference, rather than becoming a separate subject in this guide.

## 01The third atom carries away energy — 02Count events separately from consumed atoms [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

Define the event rate per volume and time by R₃ = k₃nH³. The adopted coefficient includes the counting convention; do not append an extra 1/3! factor. Each event makes one molecule and consumes two free atoms net. The third atom is present on both sides of the reaction.
dnH₂dt=k₃nH³,　dnHdt=−2k₃nH³
nN≡nH+2nH₂ = constant
nN is the hydrogen-nucleus density, counting two nuclei inside each molecule. It remains constant in a closed, fixed-volume pure-hydrogen parcel. The sum of atom and molecule counts is not conserved: forming a molecule changes the number of independently moving particles.

## 01The third atom carries away energy — 03Solve for the fraction of nuclei in molecules [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

x≡2nH₂nN,　nH=nN(1−x)
dxdt=2k₃nN²(1−x)³
Start with entirely atomic gas, x(0) = 0. Hold temperature and nucleus density fixed so k₃ is constant, then separate variables. Conversion slows as free atoms are depleted. A single initial rate therefore cannot be multiplied by an arbitrarily long time to obtain the final molecular fraction.
∫₀ˣdx′(1−x′)³=2k₃nN²t
(1−x)−2−12=2k₃nN²t
x=1−(1+4k₃nN²t)−1/2x(t₅₀)=12　⇒　t₅₀=34k₃nN²
At x = 1/2, half the nuclei reside in molecules, but the molecular number density is nN/4, not nN/2. Keep the nuclei fraction and molecular particle count distinct when interpreting both the equations and the panel.

## 01The third atom carries away energy — 04Density changes the competition with gravity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

Use equation (6) of Forrey (2013) as the rate input. TK denotes the numerical temperature in kelvins; k₃ is in cm⁶ s⁻¹. The panel restricts temperature to 600–3000 K. This fit describes the specified reaction and does not replace an entire chemical network.
TK≡T1 K,　k₃=(6×10−32TK−1/4+2×10−31TK−1/2) cm⁶ s⁻¹
tff=√3π32GmₚnN
t₅₀tff∝nN−3/2　(fixed T)
Events scale as density cubed, while the rate per atom scales as density squared. This grows faster than the inverse free-fall time, which scales as nN¹ᐟ². High density can therefore make chemistry fast relative to gravity. The displayed tff is a uniform-sphere comparison clock; the chemical parcel itself is not collapsing.

## 01The third atom carries away energy — 05Molecular formation also affects thermal evolution [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/three-body-chemistry/
Coverage: reading

Using about 4.48 eV for ground-state H₂ binding gives a binding-energy change of 4.48x/2 eV per initial nucleus. Its partition into heat, internal excitation, and later radiation requires additional physics. Since temperature is prescribed here, the panel reports only this energy scale and does not infer a temperature increase.
A realistic primordial collapse couples reverse collisional dissociation, H₂ as a third partner, low-density H⁻ pathways, cooling, and compression. Reactions omitted here become especially consequential at high temperature and late conversion. The one-way model cannot determine chemical equilibrium or a self-consistent primordial-star trajectory.
Relative to isothermal collapse, this is a small step that relaxes the assumption of fixed composition. A more complete model adds energy and fluid equations to the same nucleus bookkeeping. Identifying the conserved count and the competing clocks is a useful first step before solving that coupled system.

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tomimatsu-sato/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDerive a quadrupole difference at fixed M and J, separating an exact vacuum solution from a regular black hole.
Connect to foundations: An entry to general relativity: light, clocks, and gravity ↗

## 01An exact solution need not be a regular black hole — 01An exact solution need not be a regular black hole [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tomimatsu-sato/
Coverage: reading

Stationary, axisymmetric, asymptotically flat vacuum solutions need not be Kerr. In the Tomimatsu–Sato family, labelled by an integer δ, δ = 1 is Kerr. Focus on δ = 2 and read physical differences from its supplied Ernst potential. The assumptions differ from black-hole uniqueness theorems requiring a regular horizon.
G = c = 1,　p²+q² = 1,　p > 0,　|q| < 1
ρ = κ√[(x²−1)(1−y²)],　z = κxy
M = 2κ/p,　J = M²q
Here x > 1 and −1 ≤ y ≤ 1 are prolate spheroidal coordinates; ρ is a cylindrical coordinate, not a material density. M denotes the geometrized mass GM/c². The δ = 2 normalization differs from Kerr’s M = κ/p, so equal x values do not mean equal physical radii.

## 01An exact solution need not be a regular black hole — 02Specify the solution with a complex potential [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tomimatsu-sato/
Coverage: reading

Write the stationary axisymmetric vacuum field using the Ernst potential ℰ = f + iψ, where f = −gₜₜ and ψ is the twist potential. Supply the following polynomial solution. Complex arithmetic uses i² = −1; modulus and complex conjugation are not ordinary real squaring.
ℰ = (A−B)/(A+B)
A = p²(x⁴−1)+q²(y⁴−1)−2ipqxy(x²−y²)
B = 2px(x²−1)+2iqy(y²−1)
The vacuum equations reduce to the complex PDE below, taken as the supplied field equation. The right side uses complex squares of derivatives, not squared moduli. Numerical checks compare finite-difference residuals away from singularities and f computed from an independent metric representation.
Re(ℰ){∂x[(x²−1)∂xℰ]+∂y[(1−y²)∂yℰ]}
　= (x²−1)(∂xℰ)²+(1−y²)(∂yℰ)²

## 01An exact solution need not be a regular black hole — 03Read the quadrupole from axis data [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tomimatsu-sato/
Coverage: reading

On the upper axis y = 1, z = κx. Form ξ = (1−ℰ)/(1+ℰ) = B/A and cancel the common factor. In its asymptotic 1/z expansion, the first three coefficients give M, iJ, and the mass quadrupole Q in this convention. Higher coefficients cannot all be identified directly with relativistic multipoles.
ξaxis = 2px/[p²(x²+1)−2ipqx]
　= 2/(px)+4iq/(p²x²)−[2/p+8q²/p³]/x³+O(x−4)
　= M/z+iJ/z²+Q/z³+O(z−4)
QTS2/M³ = −(1+3q²)/4
QKerr/M³ = −q²
(QTS2−QKerr)/M³ = −(1−q²)/4
At fixed M and J, hence fixed q, the quadrupoles differ. Nonrotating TS2 retains Q = −M³/4 and is not spherical. The sign follows Manko’s equation (10). Do not translate this relativistic quadrupole directly into the axis ratio of a homogeneous Newtonian spheroid.

## 01An exact solution need not be a regular black hole — 04Locate the ring singularity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/tomimatsu-sato/
Coverage: reading

For 0 < |q| < 1 on y = 0, the condition A+B = 0 locates a ring singularity with x > 1. The polynomial below increases monotonically for x > 1, is −q² at one, and becomes positive at infinity. The panel finds its unique root by bisection.
p²x₀⁴+2px₀(x₀²−1)−1 = 0
ρring/M = (p/2)√(x₀²−1)
At q = 0 the root retreats to the boundary x = 1; the exterior-ring interpretation no longer applies. TS2 also has causal pathologies including closed timelike curves. It is not automatically an alternative model of an ordinary rotating black hole. This lesson computes the ring location without reconstructing the full causal diagram or all geodesics.

Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConstruct the thin-gas-disk dispersion relation, derive Q < 1 and the unstable band by completing the square, and distinguish linear instability from fragmentation.
Connect to foundations: The virial theorem: why shrinking stars heat up ↗

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

Unlike a uniform three-dimensional Jeans medium, a disk is thin and rotating. A local radial compression involves Coriolis and orbital responses as well as pressure. Use a razor-thin gas disk, a local approximation, axisymmetry, and constant sound speed to examine each wavelength.
κ² = 1r³d(r⁴Ω²)dr = 4Ω²+2rΩdΩdr
κ is the epicyclic frequency and equals Ω for Keplerian rotation. The density Σ below is mass per disk area, not the three-dimensional density ρ.

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 02Obtain the gravity of a thin sheet [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

For a surface-density wave δΣ exp(ikx), the potential outside the disk decays vertically as exp(−|kz|). Integrating Poisson’s equation across the sheet equates the jump of the vertical derivative to 4πGδΣ.
δΦ(z) = δΦ(0)e−|kz|
−2|k|δΦ(0) = 4πGδΣ　⇒　δΦ(0) = −2πGδΣ|k|
The wavenumber dependence differs from a uniform three-dimensional medium. This expression assumes zero thickness; a finite disk thickness weakens self-gravity at short wavelengths and requires a modified kernel.

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 03Linearize mass and two momentum equations [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

Use disturbances exp[i(kx−ωt)], with radial velocity perturbation u and azimuthal perturbation v. Continuity and the two momentum equations let us express surface-density and azimuthal perturbations in terms of u, then eliminate them.
−iωδΣ+ikΣu = 0
−iωu−2Ωv = −ik(cs²δΣΣ+δΦ)
−iωv+κ²2Ωu = 0ω² = κ²+cs²k²−2πGΣ|k|
The three terms represent rotation, pressure, and self-gravity. Rotation stabilizes long waves, while pressure stabilizes short waves. We now ask whether gravity dominates an intermediate band.

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 04Complete the square to obtain Q [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

Q ≡ csκπGΣ,　x ≡ |k|csκ
ω²κ² = 1+x²−2xQ = (x−1Q)²+1−1Q²
The minimum is 1−1/Q² at x = 1/Q. All modes have positive ω² when Q > 1. If Q < 1, a band has negative ω². At Q = 1 the mode x = 1 is neutral, with zero exponential growth rate.

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 05The unstable band and fastest-growing mode [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

For Q < 1, solve ω² = 0 to find the band edges and set ω = iσ inside the band. Maximum growth occurs at a finite wavelength between pressure and rotational stabilization, not at the longest possible wave.
x± = 1±√(1−Q²)Q,　x−<x<x+
kmax = πGΣcs²,　λmax = 2cs²GΣ
σmaxκ = √(1Q²−1)
For Q = 0.8, the unstable interval is 0.5 < x < 2, with σ = 0.75κ at x = 1.25. Applying this prediction also requires wavelengths sufficiently small compared with the disk radius for the local approximation.

## 01Rotation also resists long waves in a disk — 06Q below one does not guarantee planet formation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/toomre-q/
Coverage: reading

Compression can heat the gas and increase pressure. With slow radiative cooling, a linearly unstable disk may settle into a heated spiral or gravito-turbulent state rather than fragment. Quantities such as cooling time multiplied by Ω provide additional information about nonlinear outcomes.
Cooling thresholds depend on the equation of state, resolution, irradiation, boundary conditions, and other assumptions; there is no single universal number. Use Q as a local axisymmetric linear-stability diagnostic, not a direct assignment of fragment type or mass.

Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNFollow electron–ion temperature differences using energy conservation, and distinguish exchange and flow times.
Connect to foundations: Stars, gas, vacuum, and the forces of nature ↗
