## 01A gas need not have just one temperature — 01A gas need not have just one temperature [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/two-temperature/
Coverage: reading

Temperature measures random particle motion. Electrons and protons can each have a thermal distribution while having different temperatures. A temperature for each species already assumes internal thermalization; it does not require that the two species have finished exchanging energy with each other.
A collisionless shock can leave different species with different shares of the thermal energy. Supply the initial ratio β = Tₑ₀/Tᵢ₀ rather than deriving it from the total jump conditions. Then isolate subsequent Coulomb energy exchange from the much more complicated shock heating process.

## 01A gas need not have just one temperature — 02Energy gained by one species is lost by the other [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/two-temperature/
Coverage: reading

uₑ=32nkBTₑ,　uᵢ=32nkBTᵢ
Qₑᵢ=32nkBTᵢ−Tₑtₑᵢ
Qₑᵢ is energy received by electrons per volume per time and is positive when ions are hotter. The ions lose exactly that amount. Equal number densities give equal heat capacities, so the two temperature equations have opposite right-hand sides. Adding them checks conservation before any numerical calculation.
dTₑdt=Tᵢ−Tₑtₑᵢ,　dTᵢdt=−Tᵢ−Tₑtₑᵢ
Tₑ+Tᵢ=Tₑ₀+Tᵢ₀,　T̄=Tₑ₀+Tᵢ₀2

## 01A gas need not have just one temperature — 03Exponential relaxation and the factor of two [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/two-temperature/
Coverage: reading

Subtract the electron equation from the ion equation. Cooling of ions and heating of electrons both reduce the difference Δ = Tᵢ−Tₑ. For a constant exchange time τ, separation of variables gives a simple benchmark.
dΔdt=−2Δτ　⇒　dΔΔ=−2dtτ
Δ=Δ₀e−2t/τ,　Tₑ=T̄−Δ2,　Tᵢ=T̄+Δ2
Here τ is the exchange time in the electron equation; the difference has an e-folding time τ/2. Holding ions at a prescribed temperature would instead describe an external heat reservoir. Checking which temperatures are allowed to change prevents a factor-of-two error.

## 01A gas need not have just one temperature — 04Supply the collision time and follow relaxation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/two-temperature/
Coverage: reading

Supply the standard exchange time for a weakly coupled classical plasma. In the SI formula below, use density in m⁻³, temperatures in K, and charge in coulombs. Fix the dimensionless Coulomb logarithm at 30 instead of deriving the collision integral.
tₑᵢ=3mₑmₚ8√(2π) n [e²/(4πε₀)]² lnΛ (kBTₑmₑ+kBTᵢmₚ)3/2
When the electron thermal-speed term dominates, tₑᵢ scales as Tₑ³ᐟ²/n. Heating electrons then slows exchange, so the panel updates the coefficient rather than using a single exponential. Labels in keV mean kBT, an energy representation of temperature; 1 keV corresponds to about 1.16 × 10⁷ K.
Numerically integrate dt/dy = tₑᵢ/2 with y = ln(Δ₀/Δ), then invert the cumulative time. Simpson integration and local bisection give the temperatures while their sum is preserved analytically. The difference stays positive, and equal initial temperatures remain equal.

## 01A gas need not have just one temperature — 05Choose the relevant competing clock [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/two-temperature/
Coverage: reading

Compare the flow residence time with the near-equilibrium difference-decay time tₑᵢ(T̄)/2. A long exchange clock permits a difference to survive passage through the region. Since the coefficient changes during relaxation, inspect the trajectory as well as the single clock ratio.
The panel is a closed parcel calculation, not a simulated flow. Its residence time is an external comparison scale. Maintaining two temperatures indefinitely requires sources or sinks that distinguish species; exchange alone eventually produces one temperature.
Connect to heating in shocks and particle acceleration and electron pressure in the SZ effect. Total pressure is nkB(Tₑ+Tᵢ), whereas the electron pressure is nkBTₑ. Equal total energy therefore does not guarantee equal electron-pressure signals.

## 01From steady disks to evolving disks — 01From steady disks to evolving disks [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/viscous-disk/
Coverage: reading

The alpha chapter examined steady accretion. Now let surface density evolve in a thin Keplerian disk. A fixed central mass dominates; omit winds, external supply and disk self-gravity. Radial velocity is positive outward.
∂Σ∂t+1r∂∂r(rΣvr)=0,　j=√(GMr)
Subtract j times mass conservation from angular-momentum conservation to eliminate radial advection in favour of a stress gradient. A specified viscosity closes the model.

## 01From steady disks to evolving disks — 02Derive surface-density diffusion [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/viscous-disk/
Coverage: reading

vr=−3Σr1/2∂∂r(νΣr1/2)
∂Σ∂t=3r∂∂r[r1/2∂∂r(νΣr1/2)]
ν represents effective transport, not necessarily molecular viscosity. Prescribe ν = ν₁(r/R₁)ᵞ with γ < 2 and no dependence on time or Σ. This differs from solving temperature and transport together in a general alpha disk.
x=rR₁,　β=2−γ,　ts=R₁²3β²ν₁,　T=1+tts

## 01From steady disks to evolving disks — 03Substitute a similarity solution [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/viscous-disk/
Coverage: reading

Use zero torque at the origin, an infinite outer disk and the smooth initial profile implied below. Differentiating in τ = t/ts and y = xᵝ/T matches the powers of T and x on both sides of the PDE.
Σ(r,t)=M₀β2πR₁²x−γT−(5/2−γ)/βexp(−xβT)
This is an exact similarity solution for a particular initial profile, not for every disk. It can also provide useful late-time intuition for broader classes of initial data.
Md=M₀T−1/(2β),　Rc=R₁T1/β
Ṁin=M₀2βtsT−(5/2−γ)/β

## 01From steady disks to evolving disks — 04Inward accretion and outward spreading [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/viscous-disk/
Coverage: reading

vr=3νr[βxβT−12]
rturn=R₁[T2β]1/β
Flow is inward below rturn and outward above it. The plotted 2πr²Σ/M₀ is mass per log radius normalized to initial mass; colours denote epochs, not tracked particle trajectories.

## 01From steady disks to evolving disks — 05Account for lost mass and retained angular momentum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/viscous-disk/
Coverage: reading

Substitution y = xᵝ/T gives the disk mass integral. Angular momentum carries an extra √r factor; spreading exactly compensates the decreasing mass.
J=2π√(GM)∫0∞Σr3/2dr=constant
Maccreted=M₀−Md,　−dMddt=Ṁin
Accreted material has j → 0 at the origin, leaving angular momentum in the disk. Finite inner radii, stellar spin or winds change the budget. M₀ ≪ M lets us neglect the accreted mass in the central gravitational field.
The plots recompute this analytic solution, checked against the PDE and mass/angular-momentum budgets. They do not reproduce all initial profiles or figures in the original paper.

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 01Label particles and move their positions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Map initial Lagrangian labels q to later comoving positions x. The Zel’dovich approximation keeps the initial displacement direction and multiplies its amplitude by linear growth D. Physical position is r = ax, separating background expansion from peculiar displacement.
x(q,t)=q+D(t)s(q),　r=a(t)x
This is first-order Lagrangian perturbation theory with growing-mode initial data, not an exact general orbit solution. It primarily describes pressureless matter before shell crossing.

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 02Density follows the volume Jacobian [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

The mass of a labelled element is conserved. A small initial volume d³q maps to d³x = Jd³q; density therefore varies as the inverse determinant.
J=det(δᵢⱼ+D∂sᵢ∂qⱼ),　ρρ̄=1J
For potential displacements, let λᵢ be eigenvalues of −∂sᵢ/∂qⱼ. Each direction has its own compression factor; the largest positive eigenvalue reaches collapse first.
ρρ̄=1(1−Dλ₁)(1−Dλ₂)(1−Dλ₃)

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 03One collapsing direction gives a pancake [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Simultaneous collapse along three axes requires special symmetry. Compression along one direction first produces a sheet-like structure, hence the name pancake.
s(q)=−Aksin(kq),　x=q−DAksin(kq)
J=1−DAcos(kq),　ρρ̄=11−DAcos(kq)
The periodic one-dimensional panel uses k = 1. It displays equally spaced particle labels and the density at their mapped Eulerian positions. Playback advances D uniformly, not cosmic time.

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 04Shell crossing ends the single-stream description [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

At DA = 1 the first zero Jacobian forms a caustic. Collisionless matter can develop multiple streams; gas also needs pressure and shocks. Extending 1/J through negative Jacobians would give an unphysical negative density.

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 05Connect geometry to halo statistics [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

Zel’dovich approximates positions and shapes; Press–Schechter estimates halo statistics from smoothed fluctuations. A spherical-collapse threshold and anisotropic first shell crossing are different criteria.
A specified displacement field extends the construction to multiple dimensions, while post-crossing evolution needs methods such as N-body dynamics. Identifying where the approximation fails is part of using it correctly.

## 01Label particles and move their positions — 05Connect geometry to halo statistics [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/advanced/zeldovich/
Coverage: reading

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 02c, G, and mechanical CGS conversions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

CGS uses centimetres, grams, and seconds. Convert from SI metres, kilograms, and seconds by tracking each dimensional power. The SI value c = 299792458 m/s is exact; since 1 m = 100 cm, its CGS numerical value is one hundred times larger.
1 erg = 1 g cm² s−2 = 10−7 J
1 dyn = 1 g cm s−2 = 10−5 N
1 g/cm³ = 10³ kg/m³
1 dyn/cm² = 0.1 Pa,　1 erg/cm³ = 0.1 J/m³
From F = Gm₁m₂/r², G has dimensions length³/(mass × time²). Going from SI to CGS multiplies its numerical value by 100³/1000 = 1000.
G ≈ 6.67430×10−11 m³kg s² = 6.67430×10−8 cm³g s²

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 03Connect parsecs, light-years, and solar masses [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

The parsec originated with annual parallax and is defined as 1 pc = (648000/π) au. Since one arcsecond is π/648000 radians, this connects to the small-angle relation d ≈ 1 au/p. One au is exactly 149597870700 m.
1 pc ≈ 3.08568×1018 cm ≈ 3.26156 ly
1 kpc = 10³ pc,　1 Mpc = 10⁶ pc
1 ly = c×(365.25×86400 s) ≈ 9.46073×1017 cm
The light-year here uses the Julian year of 365.25 days. The solar mass scale, about 2×10³³ g, is useful but is not an exact universal constant in kilograms. Orbital dynamics constrains GM well, and the IAU defines a nominal solar mass parameter.
(GM)☉N = 1.3271244×1020 m³ s−2
(GM)☉NG ≈ 1.9884×1030 kg

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 04h versus ℏ, and electron mass versus energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

Frequency ν counts cycles per second, while angular frequency ω = 2πν measures phase change per second. Photon energy is hν = ℏω: the same physics with matching frequency conventions.
ℏ = h2π,　E = hν = ℏω = hcλ
hc ≈ 1239.84 eV nm,　ℏc ≈ 197.327 MeV fm
Electron rest mass is me. The value 511 keV is the rest energy mec²; the mass can be written as 511 keV/c². An electron volt is an energy unit, not a voltage.
1 eV = 1.602176634×10−19 J = 1.602176634×10−12 erg
mec² ≈ 8.18711×10−7 erg ≈ 0.510999 MeV
The fine-structure constant α is dimensionless and has the same value in SI and Gaussian units. Its vacuum, low-energy value is approximately 1/137. It is distinct from the alpha used for disk transport efficiency.
α = eSI²4πε₀ℏc = eG²ℏc ≈ 1137.036
12α²mec² ≈ 13.6 eV
The final expression estimates the hydrogen ground-state binding energy with a fixed nucleus. Electron rest energy and α thus connect to atomic photon-energy scales.

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 05Unpack the Hubble constant’s units [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

H₀ is the present expansion rate inferred from measurements and modelling, not an exact defining universal constant. Use 70 km/s/Mpc as a conversion example, not as a unique answer or a claim of current consensus.
H₀ = 70×10⁵ cm/s3.08568×10²⁴ cm ≈ 2.2685×10−18 s−1
H₀−1 ≈ 4.408×1017 s ≈ 13.97 Gyr

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 06In Gaussian CGS, convert equations as well as values [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

Electromagnetic CGS conventions include electrostatic, electromagnetic, and Gaussian systems. Here use Gaussian CGS explicitly: charge in statC and magnetic flux density B in gauss (G). Distinguish G as a magnetic unit from G as the gravitational constant.
The same physics in matched conventions
Quantity / lawSICGS–Gauss
Coulomb-force magnitudeF = |q₁q₂|/(4πε₀r²)F = |q₁q₂|/r²
Lorentz forceF⃗ = q(E⃗ + v⃗ × B⃗)F⃗ = q(E⃗ + (v⃗/c) × B⃗)
Vacuum magnetic energy densityuB = B²/(2μ₀)uB = B²/(8π)
Nonrelativistic electron gyrofrequencyωB = eB/mₑωB = eB/(mₑc)
Classical electron radiusrₑ = e²/(4πε₀mₑc²)rₑ = e²/(mₑc²)
Gaussian Coulomb’s law gives [q]² = [force][length]² = g cm³ s⁻², so charge has dimensions g1/2 cm3/2 s⁻¹. Electric and magnetic fields both have dimensions g1/2 cm−1/2 s⁻¹; v/c in the Lorentz force is dimensionless.
1 G = 10−4 T,　1 μG = 10−10 T
1 statC ≈ 3.33564×10−10 C
Match charge and field values together with the equation’s coefficients. Do not insert SI charge numbers into Gaussian equations or simply set ε₀ and μ₀ to one. In the current SI, μ₀ is measured; μ₀ ≈ 4π×10⁻⁷ H/m is an approximation.
For B = 1 G, Gaussian uB = 1/(8π) ≈ 0.03979 erg/cm³. Using 10⁻⁴ T in the SI formula gives about 0.003979 J/m³, agreeing after energy-density conversion.

## 01Start with orders of magnitude — 07Try the conversions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/appendix/numbers/
Coverage: reading

The field conversion also compares magnetic energy densities. An eV-to-K readout is the equivalent temperature from E = kBT, not an assignment of temperature to every possible particle population.

## 01Atoms have discrete energy levels [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/atom/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNCalculate the energy and wavelength of Lyman alpha from hydrogen levels.

## 01Atoms have discrete energy levels — 01Atoms have discrete energy levels [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/atom/
Coverage: reading

A hydrogen electron cannot have just any bound-state energy. We take the energy-level formula supplied by quantum theory as our starting point. The integer n is 1, 2, 3, …; zero energy corresponds to a separated electron and proton.
A level difference becomes photon energyHydrogen emits a 10.2 eV photon when it goes from n = 2 to n = 1. The horizontal lines show state energies, not electron trajectories in space.View larger diagram ↗En ≈ −13.6 eVn2A simple hydrogen model, neglecting fine structure and other small corrections.

## 01Atoms have discrete energy levels — 02What does a 2 → 1 transition emit? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/atom/
Coverage: reading

Eγ = E2 − E1 = −3.4 − (−13.6) = 10.2 eVλ = hcEγ ≈ 1240 eV·nm10.2 eV ≈ 121.6 nm
This is Lyman alpha (Lyα), an ultraviolet line strongly absorbed by Earth’s atmosphere. It is a valuable tracer of cosmic hydrogen. The 2 → 1 transition is not ionization: removing an electron from the ground state requires about 13.6 eV.

## 01Atoms have discrete energy levels — 03The same idea as the Rydberg formula [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/atom/
Coverage: reading

1λ = RH(1nlow2 − 1nhigh2)RH ≈ 1.097 × 10⁷ m⁻¹. “High” and “low” label the upper and lower levels.
Divide the energy difference by hc to obtain this expression. It is the same energy conservation statement written in terms of wavelength.

## 01Atoms have discrete energy levels — 04Atomic fingerprints on a continuous spectrum [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/atom/
Coverage: reading

Hot, optically thick matter emits a nearly thermal continuum. Foreground gas can absorb photons at selected transition energies, producing absorption lines. Emission from dilute gas can produce bright lines. Line strength depends on temperature, ionization, and radiation transport as well as abundance.

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNExplain why a limiting mass appears by comparing pressure exponents and cancelling radius.

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling — 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

A white dwarf is supported mainly by electron degeneracy pressure. The Pauli exclusion principle forbids two electrons from occupying the same full quantum state. A spatial state holds at most two opposite-spin electrons, so packing many electrons fills states up to high momenta.
What supports a white dwarf?Left: gravity and electron-pressure support. Each right-hand line represents an example spatial state; arrows indicate two spin states. Higher density requires higher momentum states. The lines are not stellar layers and do not reproduce the actual density of states.View larger diagram ↗
For number density nₑ, a spacing scale is d ∼ nₑ−1/3. Counting quantum states gives a characteristic momentum pF ∼ ℏ/d. Uncertainty offers intuition, but does not by itself derive the exclusion principle.
pF ∼ ℏne1/3,　ne = ρμemuμₑ is mass per electron in units of mᵤ. It is about 2 for fully ionized carbon or oxygen.

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling — 02Nonrelativistic electrons give P ∝ ρ5/3 [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

A pressure scale is number density × momentum × speed: P ∼ nₑpv. At low speeds, v ≈ p/mₑ. Insert p ∼ ℏnₑ1/3.
PNR ∼ nepF2me ∼ ℏ2mene5/3 ∝ ρ5/3
With ρ ∼ M/R³, PNR ∝ M5/3/R⁵, whereas PG ∼ GM²/R⁴. Shrinking makes degeneracy pressure grow faster, allowing a new equilibrium radius. At fixed composition, balancing the scalings gives R ∝ M−1/3.

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling — 03Relativity changes the exponent [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

E2 = p2c2 + me2c4
This energy–momentum relation is an input from special relativity. At p ≫ mₑc, E ≈ pc and v ≈ c. Momentum keeps increasing while speed approaches c. The pressure scaling changes to:
PUR ∼ nepFc ∼ ℏc ne4/3 ∼ ℏc(μemu)4/3M4/3R4

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling — 04Cancel radius to expose a mass scale [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

ℏc M4/3(μemu)4/3R4 ∼ GM2R4M2/3 ∼ ℏcG(μemu)4/3　⇒　M ∼ (ℏcG)3/21(μemu)2
Divide by M4/3, then raise both sides to the power 3/2. Both pressures scale as R⁻⁴, so changing the radius no longer changes their ratio. This is the central reason a limiting mass appears.

## 01Electron degeneracy pressure survives cooling — 05Where does the coefficient near 1.4 solar masses come from? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/chandra/
Coverage: reading

MCh ≈ 5.83μe2M☉ ≈ 1.46 (2μe)2M☉
With μₑ defined using the atomic mass constant, this ideal model gives about 1.46 M☉, commonly summarized as “about 1.4 M☉.” Different conventions and corrections also lead to values such as 1.44. Composition, electron capture, nuclear reactions, general relativity, and rotation affect real evolution.
Crossing a limiting mass does not guarantee one particular explosion. Composition and accretion history help determine whether thermonuclear runaway, collapse, or another outcome occurs.

## 01Light carries information [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/distance/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNExplain how luminosity differs from apparent brightness and how light reveals distance.

## 01Light carries information — 01Light carries information [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/distance/
Coverage: reading

Radio waves, infrared, visible light, ultraviolet, and X-rays are all electromagnetic radiation. In vacuum they travel at c ≈ 3.00 × 10⁸ m/s. Wavelength λ and frequency ν obey c = λν. One photon carries E = hν = hc/λ.
E[eV] ≈ 1240λ[nm]A 500 nm photon carries about 2.48 eV. Shorter wavelengths mean higher photon energies.

## 01Light carries information — 02Spread the light over a sphere [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/distance/
Coverage: reading

Luminosity L is the total energy a star emits per second. If it radiates uniformly, that energy crosses a sphere of area 4πd². The flux f is the power received per square metre.
Light spreads over a growing sphereLight from a star of fixed luminosity spreads over spheres. At 2d the area is four times larger, so the flux per receiving area is one quarter. The spheres are schematic.View larger diagram ↗f = L4πd2　⇒　d = √(L4πf)This assumes no absorption and isotropic emission. Luminosity is in W; flux in W/m².

## 01Light carries information — 03Triangulation for nearby stars [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/distance/
Coverage: reading

As Earth orbits the Sun, a nearby star shifts against distant background objects. Its annual parallax p corresponds to a baseline of 1 au: half the maximum shift measured six months apart. One parsec is the distance corresponding to one arcsecond.
Parallax as a triangleParallax p corresponds to a baseline of 1 au from Sun to Earth. Earth positions six months apart are about 2 au apart, giving a maximum angular shift of about 2p. Angles and body sizes are exaggerated.View larger diagram ↗d[pc] = 1p[arcsec]A parallax of 0.05 arcseconds gives 20 pc, about 65 light-years. A light-year is a distance.

## 01Light carries information — 04A ladder of calibrations [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/distance/
Coverage: reading

Solar-system ranging → stellar parallax → period–luminosity relations such as Cepheids → Type Ia supernovae. Nearby distances calibrate the intrinsic brightness used at the next stage. Type Ia supernovae must be standardized using their light-curve shapes and colours; their luminosities are not identical.
v ≈ H0d,　z ≈ vcApproximations for the nearby universe at small redshift. H₀ must be calibrated using other distance measurements or a cosmological model.
Continue to cosmic expansion and Hubble’s law → See why uniform fractional growth makes recession speed proportional to distance.

## 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/eddington/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNBalance forces and see why r² cancels, giving LEdd = 4πGMc/κ.

## 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy — 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/eddington/
Coverage: reading

pγ = Eγc,　f = L4πr2
Matter gains momentum by absorbing or scattering radiation. For Thomson scattering, angular averaging lets us use the flux and effective cross section for the net radial momentum transfer. Electric fields couple electrons to ions, so the gas moves together.

## 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy — 02Start with an electron–proton pair [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/eddington/
Coverage: reading

Frad = σTL4πr2c,　Fgrav ≈ GMmpr2M is the central mass; the surrounding gas is fully ionized pure hydrogen. Electron mass is negligible compared with proton mass.

## 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy — 03Equate the forces and cancel r² [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/eddington/
Coverage: reading

σTL4πr2c = GMmpr2σTL = 4πGMmpc　⇒　LEdd = 4πGMmpcσT
For one solar mass this is approximately 1.26 × 10³¹ W, or 3.3 × 10⁴ L☉, scaling linearly with M. We obtained the coefficient from a force balance without solving a differential equation.

## 01Photons carry momentum as well as energy — 04Use opacity for a different composition [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/eddington/
Coverage: reading

arad = κL4πr2c,　agrav = GMr2LEdd = 4πGMcκ

## 01The hot early universe [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNEstimate the helium mass fraction from the neutron-to-proton ratio.

## 01The hot early universe — 01The hot early universe [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

In the early universe, reactions converted protons and neutrons into one another. Expansion and cooling made those reactions fall behind, and some neutrons decayed. Near the onset of efficient nucleosynthesis, a useful estimate is neutron : proton ≈ 1 : 7. Predicting that ratio requires weak-interaction and expansion calculations.

## 01The hot early universe — 02Two neutrons and two protons make helium-4 [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

Take 2 neutrons and 14 protons. If almost all neutrons end up in helium-4, one helium nucleus forms and 12 protons remain. Neglecting nucleon mass differences and binding energies, helium contains 4 of the 16 mass units.
Yp ≈ 416 = 0.25Yp is the primordial helium mass fraction in ordinary matter, not 25% of the universe’s total energy.Yp ≈ 2q1+q,　q = NnNpNn neutrons make Nn/2 helium nuclei, containing 2Nn nucleons in total.

## 01The hot early universe — 03Stars build beyond the primordial elements [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

Primordial nucleosynthesis produced mainly hydrogen and helium, with traces including deuterium, helium-3, and lithium. Stars turn hydrogen into more helium, then later reactions produce carbon and oxygen. Massive-star evolution can build nuclei near the iron group.
Elements heavier than iron have several origins, including slow neutron capture in evolved stars and rapid neutron capture in extreme environments such as neutron-star mergers. One kind of supernova does not explain every heavy element.

## 01The hot early universe — 04Reading light reveals the history of matter [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

Atomic levels produce lines; line ratios and widths reveal temperature, composition, and motion. Luminosity and distance reveal energy output. Gravity and quantum physics set the conditions under which a star can exist. The equations connect different aspects of the same universe.

## 01The hot early universe — 04Reading light reveals the history of matter [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/elements/
Coverage: reading

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNObtain v = HD from a scale factor and distinguish redshift, Hubble time, and cosmic age.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

In the standard large-scale model, the universe is approximately homogeneous and isotropic. For points following the cosmic flow, write their same-time proper separation as D(t) = a(t)χ, using an appropriate fixed comoving separation χ. The dimensionless scale factor a(t) is conventionally one today.
Galaxy separations grow by the same factorAn example of galaxy separations increasing uniformly by 1.5, with galaxy sizes held fixed. The grid compares distances; it is not a physical framework or a special centre.View larger diagram ↗D = aχ,　D' = a'χ　⇒　D′D = a′aPrimes label values at a different time; they do not denote derivatives here.
If a grows from 1 to 1.1, separations of points following the cosmic flow grow by 1.1. This does not make galaxies, solar systems, or atoms expand by the same factor: binding forces determine their internal structures.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 02Divide the change by time to obtain Hubble’s law [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

During a short interval Δt, let the scale factor increase by Δa. Since χ stays fixed, ΔD = χΔa = DΔa/a. Divide by Δt to find the rate of separation.
ΔDΔt = (ΔaaΔt)DHere a and D are evaluated at the start of the interval. For fixed χ this finite-interval equation is an equality.HΔt ≡ ΔaaΔt,　vΔt ≡ ΔDΔt　⇒　vΔt = HΔtD
For instantaneous rates the relation is vrec = H(t)D. This is the Hubble–Lemaître relation for uniform expansion. H(t) is a fractional growth rate per unit time; H₀ is its present value. The name “Hubble constant” does not make it constant throughout cosmic history.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 03Every observer sees more distant points separate faster [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

Imagine A, B, and C on a line with every gap growing by 1% in a short time. If C is twice as far from A as B is, its separation increases twice as much. From B, both sides follow the same rule. This does not identify Earth as a special centre of expansion.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 04Stretched wavelengths give cosmological redshift [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

z ≡ λobs−λemitλemit　⇒　1+z = λobsλemit
For emission and observation by comoving observers in a homogeneous, isotropic universe, general relativity supplies the following relation. It is an additional result about light propagating through expanding spacetime, not an algebraic consequence of the definition of z alone.
1+z = anowaemit　⇒　aemit = 11+z　(anow=1)
At z = 1, the emission-era scale factor was 0.5. At high redshift, inserting z into a special-relativistic Doppler formula is not a general way to infer cosmic distance. Observations can also include peculiar velocities and gravitational redshifts.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 05Infer distances in the nearby-universe approximation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

z ≈ vc,　v ≈ H0D　⇒　D ≈ czH0
Use H₀ = 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹ as a round teaching example, not a claim that current measurements have settled on a unique value. One megaparsec is about 3.26 million light-years. At 10 Mpc the example gives 700 km/s; at 100 Mpc, 7,000 km/s.
For z = 0.005, v ≈ 1,500 km/s and D ≈ 21.4 Mpc. Peculiar motion can substantially affect nearby-galaxy estimates. At large distances the expansion history and the definition of distance matter, so this approximation is insufficient.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 061/H₀ is a timescale, not automatically the age [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

H0 ≈ 70 km/s3.086×1019 km ≈ 2.27×10−18 s−1tH ≡ 1H0 ≈ 4.41×1017 s ≈ 14 billion yearsThe Hubble time for the example H₀ = 70 km s⁻¹ Mpc⁻¹.
Dividing the present separation D by the present recession rate H₀D gives 1/H₀. But that separation need not have grown at its present rate throughout the past. Determining the cosmic age requires the expansion history.

## 01Multiply galaxy separations by a common factor — 061/H₀ is a timescale, not automatically the age [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/expansion/
Coverage: reading

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNExplain charge cancellation and why gravity adds up.

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? — 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

Space between stars contains atoms, ions, electrons, dust, and radiation. Even a very dilute medium can absorb or scatter light over a long path. Hot stellar interiors are plasmas: electrons are no longer bound to individual atoms.

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? — 02Compare two inverse-square laws [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

FG = Gmp2r2,　FE = e24πε0r2FEFG = e24πε0Gmp2 ≈ 1.2 × 1036e is the elementary charge, ε₀ the vacuum permittivity, and mₚ the proton mass. This ratio is for two protons.

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? — 03Large objects are nearly electrically neutral [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

Nearly equal positive and negative charges largely cancel the long-range electric force. Gravity from ordinary matter adds attractively. This makes gravity central to the global balance of stars, while magnetic fields and electromagnetic forces still matter in many cosmic settings.

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? — 04Pressure is force per area [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

P = FA,　Pgas = nkBTn is the number of particles per unit volume. The ideal-gas equation describes pressure from particle motion.
Pressure itself has no direction. A star is supported by the force imbalance created when pressure is higher inside than outside.

## 01Is a cosmic vacuum really empty? — 04Pressure is force per area [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/matter/
Coverage: reading

## 01Specify the quantity to be found [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNSolve detailed balance for uν and identify where the denominator’s −1 comes from.

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 01Specify the quantity to be found [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

uν is radiation energy density per unit frequency, in J m⁻³ Hz⁻¹. A narrow interval Δν contains approximately uνΔν of energy density. The two levels are separated by hν; n₁ and n₂ are their number densities and g₁ and g₂ their statistical weights.

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 02Write down the three processes [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

Absorption: n₁B₁₂uν. Spontaneous emission: n₂A₂₁. Stimulated emission: n₂B₂₁uν. When atoms and radiation share thermal equilibrium, the upward and downward transition rates balance.
n1B12uν = n2A21 + n2B21uν

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 03Collect the uν terms and divide [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

(n1B12−n2B21)uν = n2A21uν = A21/B21(n1/n2)(B12/B21)−1
In the second line we divided numerator and denominator by n₂B₂₁. So far this only rearranges the balance condition; it does not yet determine the spectrum.

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 04Insert the Boltzmann ratio and Einstein relations [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

n1n2 = g1g2ehν/(kBT),　g1B12 = g2B21A21B21 = 8πhν3c3
The B relation reflects microscopic transition symmetry. The A/B ratio is linked to electromagnetic mode counting and can also be fixed by matching the classical Rayleigh–Jeans limit. These are additional physical inputs; detailed balance alone does not determine the factor 8π.

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 05Arrive at Planck’s law [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

uν(T) = 8πhν3c31exp(hν/kBT)−1
At low frequency, x = hν/(kBT) ≪ 1 and eˣ − 1 ≈ x. Substitution gives uν ≈ 8πν²kBT/c³, the Rayleigh–Jeans result. At high frequency the exponential suppresses the spectrum.
Bν = c4πuνFor isotropic thermal radiation. Specific intensity Bν, energy density uν, and surface flux have different units.

## 01Specify the quantity to be found — 05Arrive at Planck’s law [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/planck/
Coverage: reading

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDistinguish Newtonian estimates from results supplied by general relativity.

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras — 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

Imagine a clock made of light bouncing between mirrors. In its rest frame, a one-way trip covers ℓ in t₀ = ℓ/c. An observer seeing the clock move sideways at speed v sees a diagonal light path. Both measure light speed c.
A moving light clock makes a triangleCompare one-way trips between mirrors. The right panel shows the lower mirror at departure and upper mirror at arrival, with horizontal travel vt and mirror spacing ℓ. At the same light speed c, the longer diagonal path takes a longer inertial-frame time t.View larger diagram ↗(ct)2 = ℓ2 + (vt)2　⇒　t = t0√(1−v2/c2)
For these same two clock events, the inertial-frame interval t exceeds the moving clock’s proper interval t₀. This is special-relativistic time dilation, distinct from gravitational time dilation.

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras — 02Estimate escape speed using Newtonian mechanics [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

12mvesc2 = GMmR　⇒　vesc = √(2GMR)
Formally setting vesc = c yields R = 2GM/c². But mv²/2 is not the kinetic energy of a photon. This is a clue to a length scale, not a derivation of a relativistic horizon.

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras — 03The relativistic result: Schwarzschild radius [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

rs = 2GMc2 ≈ 2.95 (MM☉) km
General relativity describes gravity through spacetime geometry. Its solution for a nonrotating, uncharged, spherical black hole identifies this radius as an event horizon. Future-directed light and matter inside cannot reach the distant exterior. Deriving that spacetime solution is outside our scope.

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras — 04Compare stationary clocks in a gravitational field [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

ΔτΔt = √(1−rsr)　(r > rs)Δτ is the time recorded by a stationary clock at r; Δt corresponds to a stationary clock far away.
This equation is supplied by the relativistic solution. At r = 2rs, the ratio is √(1/2) ≈ 0.707. During an interval corresponding to one hour far away, the stationary local clock advances about 42.4 minutes.

## 01Special relativity: a light clock and Pythagoras — 04Compare stationary clocks in a gravitational field [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/relativity/
Coverage: reading

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNConnect the scale rₑ to σT and then to opacity κ.

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales — 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/
Coverage: reading

Define rₑ as the length at which the electric energy scale e²/(4πε₀r) equals the electron rest energy mₑc². Multiply by r, then divide by mₑc².
An accelerated electron scatters lightIn the classical picture of Thomson scattering, the incident electric field accelerates an electron, which radiates. Two outgoing rays illustrate possible directions; they do not imply photon splitting or isotropic scattering.View larger diagram ↗e24πε0re = mec2　⇒　re = e24πε0mec2 ≈ 2.82 × 10−15 m

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales — 02Light shakes an electron, which radiates [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/
Coverage: reading

Let the incident field be E(t) = E₀ cos(2πνt). A free electron has acceleration amplitude a₀ = eE₀/mₑ. Since the cycle average of cos² is 1/2, ⟨a²⟩ = e²E₀²/(2mₑ²).
⟨P⟩ = e2⟨a2⟩6πε0c3 = e4E0212πε0me2c3We take the nonrelativistic Larmor radiation law as an input rather than deriving it here.

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales — 03Cross section = scattered power / incident flux [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/
Coverage: reading

The mean incident flux is I = ε₀cE₀²/2. A scattering cross section is an effective area: it tells us how much incident power is redirected, rather than measuring a little solid ball.
σT = ⟨P⟩I = e46πε02me2c4 = 8π3re2σT ≈ 6.65 × 10−29 m2E₀² cancels. In this weak-field limit the cross section depends on neither intensity nor frequency.

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales — 04From one electron to one kilogram of gas [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/
Coverage: reading

κe = neσTρρ is mass density and nₑ electron number density. Opacity κ has units m²/kg.
For fully ionized pure hydrogen, one electron accompanies each proton, so ρ ≈ nₑmₚ and κₑ ≈ σT/mₚ ≈ 0.040 m²/kg. For a hydrogen–helium mixture with hydrogen mass fraction X, κₑ ≈ 0.020(1 + X) m²/kg, or about 0.034 at X ≈ 0.7.

## 01Compare electric and rest-energy scales — 04From one electron to one kilogram of gas [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/scattering/

## 01Estimate the pressure required for support [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/structure/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNFollow the algebra leading to L ∝ M³ and state its assumptions.

## 01Estimate the pressure required for support — 01Estimate the pressure required for support [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/structure/
Coverage: reading

Treat a star of mass M and radius R as a single region, omitting geometric coefficients. A gravitational force scale is GM²/R². Divide by an area of order R² to estimate the supporting pressure.
Inside a star, with light escapingTemperature and pressure generally rise toward the centre. The light path is schematic. The circular boundaries are explanatory, not a detailed depiction of stellar layers or convection.View larger diagram ↗ρ ∼ MR3,　Pc ∼ GM2R4
The star needs higher pressure toward its centre. These are representative scale estimates, not a solution for the internal profiles.

## 01Estimate the pressure required for support — 02For gas-pressure support, central temperature scales as M/R [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/structure/
Coverage: reading

Pc ∼ ρkBTcμmu　⇒　Tc ∼ μmuGMkBR
μ is the mean mass per gas particle in units of the atomic mass constant mᵤ. The right-hand side has units of kelvin. At fixed composition, Tc ∝ M/R. This central temperature is different from the surface effective temperature Teff.
The virial theorem chapter obtains the same M/R scaling from the star’s global energy.

## 01Estimate the pressure required for support — 03Luminosity from radiative diffusion [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/structure/
Coverage: reading

In a nearly thermal interior, radiation energy density scales as aTc⁴, giving stored radiation energy of order aTc⁴R³. Divide by tdiff ∼ R²/(cℓ) and insert ℓ = 1/(κρ). Here a is the radiation constant.
L ∼ acTc4Rℓ ∼ acRTc4κρ ∼ acR4Tc4κM

## 01Estimate the pressure required for support — 04The fourth powers of R cancel [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/structure/
Coverage: reading

Extension: nuclear temperature sensitivity and stellar radius
Approximate energy production per unit mass by ε ∝ ρTcη. Then Lnuc ∼ Mε ∝ Mη + 2/Rη + 3. Equate this to L ∝ M³ in the same constant-opacity model.
Rη+3 ∝ Mη−1　⇒　R ∝ M(η−1)/(η+3)Teff ∝ (LR2)1/4 ∝ M(η+11)/[4(η+3)]
For η ≈ 16 this gives R ∝ M0.79 and Teff ∝ M0.355. These are restricted-model scalings, not precision formulas for the whole main sequence. Changing the opacity law requires redoing the derivation.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNCalculate effective temperature and distinguish inference from a theoretical prediction.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” — 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

The Sun has no solid surface. Its photosphere is the layer from which visible light escapes. Its effective temperature is about 5,772 K, or about 5,500°C. “About 6,000 degrees” is a rough statement; 6,000 K and 6,000°C are different.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” — 02Total power = area × power per unit area [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

F = σSBT4,　L = 4πR2σSBTeff4σSB ≈ 5.670 × 10⁻⁸ W m⁻² K⁻⁴ is the Stefan–Boltzmann constant, distinct from σT.Teff = (L4πR2σSB)1/4
Insert L☉ = 3.828 × 10²⁶ W and R☉ = 6.957 × 10⁸ m to obtain about 5,772 K. Effective temperature means the temperature of a blackbody with the same radius and luminosity, even if the actual spectrum is not exactly blackbody.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” — 03How much of “why” have we answered? [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

Measured luminosity and radius determine effective temperature. Predicting luminosity and radius from mass and composition requires a stellar model combining gravity, nuclear reactions, radiation transport, and convection. A mean free path alone cannot select 6,000 degrees.
Inside the Sun, absorption, emission, scattering, and energy transport happen repeatedly. It is insufficient to picture a single core photon merely cooling on its way out. Escape becomes likely where the outward optical depth is of order unity.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” — 04A second thermometer: the spectral shape [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

λmaxT ≈ 2.898 × 10−3 m·KWien’s displacement law for the peak of the wavelength spectrum Bλ.
At 5,772 K the Bλ peak is near 502 nm. The Sun emits a broad spectrum, not just green light. Converting the peak of Bν using c/ν does not give the Bλ peak, because the spectral density is defined per a different variable.

## 01Clarify “surface” and “degrees” — 04A second thermometer: the spectral shape [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/sun/
Coverage: reading

## 01The distance between interactions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNCalculate a mean free path and follow why a heat bath produces Boltzmann factors.

## 01The distance between interactions — 01The distance between interactions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

If scatterers have uniform number density n and cross section σ, a narrow path of length x encounters an expected nσx targets. Set nσℓ = 1 to find the mean free path.
ℓ = 1nσ = 1κρ,　τopt = Rℓ = κρRThe second expression is the optical depth of a uniform medium. We write τopt to distinguish it from a lifetime.

## 01The distance between interactions — 02Many scatterings make a random walk [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

After N random steps, the net displacement is roughly √N ℓ. Escaping a region of radius R needs N ∼ (R/ℓ)² steps. Each takes ℓ/c, giving the following diffusion-time estimate.
A photon’s random walkOne possible path through many scatterings. Pale circles indicate scatterers and dark dots indicate changes of direction. The step lengths vary; their mean is the mean free path. Distinguish total path length from net displacement.View larger diagram ↗tdiff ∼ R2cℓ　(ℓ ≪ R)

## 01The distance between interactions — 03An isolated whole, with a subsystem exchanging heat [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

For an isolated “small system + large heat bath,” count the microstates at fixed total energy equally: the microcanonical starting point. The probability that the small system has energy Eᵢ is proportional to the bath’s number of states at Etot − Eᵢ.
S = kB ln Ω,　ΔSbath ≈ −EiT
We use the thermodynamic relation that a large bath losing energy Eᵢ loses approximately Eᵢ/T of entropy while its temperature stays nearly fixed. Divide by kB and exponentiate to obtain the ratio of state counts.
pi ∝ gi exp(−EikBT)gᵢ counts states with the same energy: their statistical weight. This is the canonical distribution of the subsystem.

## 01The distance between interactions — 04For two levels, simply take a ratio [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

n2n1 = g2g1exp(−E2−E1kBT)
For a 10.2 eV separation at 10⁴ K, kBT ≈ 0.862 eV, giving exp(−11.8) ≈ 7.2 × 10⁻⁶. Thermal excitation is scarce. The overall ionization fraction requires a separate calculation.

## 01The distance between interactions — 04For two levels, simply take a ratio [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/thermal/
Coverage: reading

Extension: entropy maximization with multipliers α and β
This optional part uses derivatives, but no differential equations. For classical independent particles with populations nᵢ, Ω = N! ∏gᵢⁿⁱ/nᵢ!. At large populations use Stirling’s approximation: ln n! ≈ n ln n − n.
F = ln Ω − α(Σni−N) − β(ΣniEi−U)
Maximize lnΩ while keeping Σnᵢ = N and ΣnᵢEᵢ = U fixed. Differentiating with respect to each nᵢ gives −ln(nᵢ/gᵢ) − α − βEᵢ = 0. Exponentiate to solve.
ni = gie−αe−βEi,　β = 1kBT
Identifying β uses the thermodynamic entropy–temperature relation; α normalizes particle number. Applying this classical counting directly to photons does not yield Planck’s law: photons are bosons with nonconserved number. The next chapter uses Einstein coefficients instead.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDistinguish equalities from estimates and calculate how a quantity scales.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 01Start with “by what factor?” [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

A sphere has area A = 4πR². Replace R by 2R to find how the area changes. Ratios like this are one of the most useful tools in astrophysics.
A' = 4π(2R)2 = 4 × 4πR2 = 4A
The notation a ∝ bⁿ means a = Cbⁿ with the constant C omitted. Check that C stays fixed before using a scaling relation.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 02Combine powers and clear denominators [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

R4(MR)4 = R4M4R4 = M4
You may multiply or divide both sides by the same quantity, provided you never divide by zero. A fractional power M1/3 is a cube root; M2/3 is its square.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 03Four symbols with different jobs [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

= is an equality within a model; ≈ an approximation; ∼ an order-of-magnitude estimate with coefficients omitted; ∝ a scaling under fixed conditions. An estimate cannot determine an exact coefficient.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 04Units are part of the equation [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

We generally use SI: metres, seconds, kilograms, kelvin, joules, and watts (joules per second). Useful reference scales are M☉ ≈ 1.99 × 10³⁰ kg and L☉ ≈ 3.83 × 10²⁶ W.
1 eV = 1.602 × 10−19 JThe electronvolt is a unit of energy. Temperature conversions use T[K] = t[°C] + 273.15.
exp(x) means eˣ, with e ≈ 2.718. The natural logarithm reverses it: ln(eˣ) = x. If these functions are new to you, start with the numerical examples.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 05Keep these constants at hand [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

c: speed of light. G: gravitational constant. h: Planck’s constant, converting photon frequency to energy. ℏ = h/(2π): reduced Planck constant. kB: Boltzmann’s constant, converting temperature to an energy scale.
G ≈ 6.674 × 10−11 m3kg−1s−2
h ≈ 6.626 × 10−34 J·s
kB ≈ 1.381 × 10−23 J/K ≈ 8.617 × 10−5 eV/K
mₑ ≈ 9.109 × 10⁻³¹ kg is the electron mass; mₚ ≈ 1.673 × 10⁻²⁷ kg is the proton mass; mᵤ ≈ 1.661 × 10⁻²⁷ kg is the atomic mass constant. The elementary charge e ≈ 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C is distinct from the base e of exponential functions; context identifies which is meant.

## 01Start with “by what factor?” — 05Keep these constants at hand [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/tools/
Coverage: reading

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNUse 2K + U = 0 to estimate temperature and track the energy released in contraction.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

Let a small mass m orbit a dominant mass M in a circle of radius r at speed v. Gravity supplies the centripetal force: mv²/r = GMm/r². Multiply by r to obtain mv² = GMm/r.
K = mv22 = GMm2r,　U = −GMmr　⇒　2K + U = 0K is kinetic energy and U is gravitational potential energy, with zero at infinite separation.
For a circular orbit this holds at each instant. Extending it to elliptical orbits and many particles requires averaging and conditions.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 02Use the general theorem as a physical input [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

2⟨K⟩ + ⟨U⟩ = 0
For a gravitationally bound system whose overall extent does not keep growing or shrinking, this relation holds under suitable time averaging. Angle brackets denote time averages. We use the general result as an input, with the circular-orbit example providing intuition.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 03Estimate an internal temperature from gravity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

Write the gravitational energy as U ≈ −αGM²/R, with a dimensionless structure coefficient α depending on the density profile. Let N = M/(μmᵤ), and use a mean kinetic energy 3kBT̄/2 per particle. T̄ is a particle-number-weighted mean temperature.
2K = 3NkBT̄ ≈ αGM2R3MμmukBT̄ ≈ αGM2R　⇒　T̄ ≈ αμmuGM3kBR
At fixed composition and structure coefficient, T̄ ∝ M/R: halving the radius approximately doubles the mean temperature. Using the Sun’s M and R, μ ≈ 0.6, and an illustrative α = 1 gives about 4.6 million K. This is an internal temperature scale, not the central temperature or the 5,772 K surface effective temperature.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 04Total energy is minus the thermal energy [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

U = −2K　⇒　E = K+U = −K = U2
Watch the signs. A bound star has negative total energy. If it radiates while maintaining this balance at fixed mass and composition, E decreases while K increases.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 05Radiating while heating up: negative heat capacity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

ΔE = −Q　⇒　ΔK = Q,　ΔU = −2Q　(Q > 0)
This describes quasi-static contraction without an energy source such as nuclear burning. Gravitational energy decreases by 2Q: Q raises the thermal energy, and Q escapes as radiation. The half-and-half split follows from the signs in the energy balance.
A contracting star can radiate and heat upA schematic quasi-statically contracting star supported by nonrelativistic ideal gas. Particle arrows indicate a thermal-motion scale. The decrease in gravitational energy supplies both heating and radiation.View larger diagram ↗
Continue to stellar structure and mass–luminosity scaling → Connect the gravitational temperature scale to the transport of light.

## 01Begin with one body in a circular orbit — 05Radiating while heating up: negative heat capacity [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/virial/
Coverage: reading

## 01First define the width [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/width/
Coverage: reading

WHAT YOU WILL LEARNDistinguish natural and thermal broadening and calculate temperature scaling.

## 01First define the width — 01First define the width [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/width/
Coverage: reading

We use the full width at half maximum (FWHM): the full separation between the two half-height points. Standard deviations and half-widths have different numerical factors; always specify the convention.

## 01First define the width — 02Finite lifetimes produce natural broadening [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/width/
Coverage: reading

A wave lasting only a finite time has a spread of frequencies, unlike an endless monochromatic wave. A shorter excited-state lifetime gives a broader natural line. We use the following result from wave analysis.
Δνnat,FWHM = 12πτFor a long-lived lower level and negligible additional broadening. The natural profile is Lorentzian.

## 01First define the width — 03Atoms move toward and away from us [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/width/
Coverage: reading

Δνν0 ≈ vlosc,　σv = √(kBTm)vlos is positive toward the observer; σv is the one-dimensional velocity standard deviation. We assume |v| ≪ c.Δνth,FWHM = ν0c√(8 ln 2 · kBTm)
A Gaussian distribution of line-of-sight thermal velocities gives a Gaussian frequency profile. Its FWHM is 2√(2 ln 2) ≈ 2.355 times the standard deviation.

## 01First define the width — 04Compare the widths for hydrogen at 10,000 K [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/chapter/width/
Coverage: reading

Hydrogen has σᵥ ≈ 9.1 km/s and a thermal FWHM of about 21 km/s. At Lyα, ν₀ ≈ 2.47 × 10¹⁵ Hz, so Δνth ≈ 1.8 × 10¹¹ Hz. A 2p lifetime of about 1.6 ns gives a natural width near 1.0 × 10⁸ Hz.
Thermal broadening dominates the core in this example, but Lorentzian wings decline slowly and can still matter far from line centre. Combining the two gives a Voigt profile. Collisions, turbulence, rotation, and instruments can add further broadening.

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Ostriker’s isothermal cylinder and critical line mass [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Exact analytic equilibrium
ρ(r) = ρc[1 + r²/(8H²)]−2,　H² = cₛ²/(4πGρc)
Infinite, axisymmetric, isothermal cylinder with no rotation or magnetic field.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataFinite density and zero gradient on the axis; optional truncation at radius R by external pressure.Free parametersSound speed, central density, and truncated line-mass fraction.Validity and caveatsThe limiting line mass is 2cₛ²/G. Total mass of an infinite filament and finite-filament ends are different issues.Read the derivation and explore ↗Ostriker (1964) · The equilibrium of polytropic and isothermal cylinders, §III, equations (56)–(61) ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Gravitational potentials of ellipsoids [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Exact potential of a homogeneous ellipsoid
Φ = πGρ(A₁x² + A₂y² + A₃z²) + constant
A prescribed homogeneous ellipsoid; first compute its interior gravitational field.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataFinite interior field and continuous matching to the exterior potential; axis integrals determine the coefficients.Free parametersSemiaxes a, b, c and density.Validity and caveatsAn arbitrarily prescribed ellipsoid need not be a rotating fluid equilibrium. See the tensor-virial chapter for equilibrium sequences.Read the derivation and explore ↗Fitzpatrick · Hydrostatics: ellipsoidal potentials and rotating figures ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Tensor virial theorem and Maclaurin/Jacobi sequences [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Analytic conditions and numerical equilibrium sequences
Maclaurin: a = b;　Jacobi: a > b > c
Homogeneous incompressible self-gravitating fluid in uniform rotation with a free surface.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataZero surface pressure; total gravitational and centrifugal potential is constant on the surface.Free parametersAxis ratios, angular speed, and density.Validity and caveatsEquilibrium bifurcation, secular instability, and dynamical instability differ. Do not directly apply incompressible thresholds to compressible stars.Read the derivation and explore ↗Lebovitz (1961) · The virial tensor and rotating figures ↗

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Steady transonic solution
Ṁ = 4πr²ρv;　rₛ = GM/(2cₛ²)　(isothermal)
Steady spherical inflow dominated by a central mass; the panel is isothermal.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataSpecify far-field density and sound speed; select the inflow branch passing smoothly through the sonic point.Free parametersCentral mass, ambient density, and sound speed.Validity and caveatsOmit rotation, magnetic fields, and radiative feedback. Other stationary branches need not cross the sonic point.Read the derivation and explore ↗Bondi (1952) · On spherically symmetrical accretion ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Parker wind and transonic outflow [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Steady transonic solution
(v − cₛ²/v) dv/dr = 2cₛ²/r − GM/r²
Steady spherical isothermal outflow; the mechanism maintaining temperature is supplied.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataJoin an inner subsonic flow to an outer supersonic flow through the sonic point; supply density normalization separately.Free parametersCentral mass, sound speed, and mass flux or reference density.Validity and caveatsSimilar equations to Bondi, but different branches and boundary conditions. Omit the actual solar wind’s thermal and magnetic structure.Read the derivation and explore ↗Parker (1958) · Dynamics of the interplanetary gas and magnetic fields ↗

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

First-kind similarity; numerically integrated profiles
R(t) = β(Et²/ρ₀)1/5
Point explosion into uniform gas with an ideal equation of state and a strong adiabatic shock.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataShock jumps at the outer boundary, interior regularity, and total-energy normalization.Free parametersExplosion energy, ambient density, adiabatic index, and time.Validity and caveatsThe radius scaling alone does not determine the profiles. Excludes ejecta-dominated and strongly radiative stages.Read the derivation and explore ↗Ohio State · Spherical blastwaves and supernova remnants ↗

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Numerical similarity solutions
x = r/(cₛt),　ρ = α(x)/(4πGt²),　Ṁ = m₀cₛ³/G
Spherical isothermal self-gravity; displayed profiles are after central formation.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataShu collapse joins a singular isothermal envelope; LPH is a distinct continuation with a moving envelope.Free parametersSound speed, time, and the family specified by outer density and velocity.Validity and caveatsIdentical similarity variables do not imply identical accretion coefficients. Rounded published LPH outer coefficients are supplied inputs.Read the derivation and explore ↗Shu (1977) · Self-similar collapse of isothermal spheres ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Lynden–Bell–Pringle viscous-disk similarity solutions [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Analytic similarity for a prescribed viscosity
∂Σ/∂t = (3/r)∂[r1/2∂(νΣr1/2)/∂r]/∂r
Thin axisymmetric Keplerian disk with ν ∝ rᵞ and no external torque.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataZero torque at the origin, an infinite outer domain, and finite mass and angular momentum.Free parametersViscosity exponent γ, initial mass and length scales, and dimensionless time.Validity and caveatsViscosity is prescribed. Does not directly solve MRI turbulence or angular-momentum loss in a wind.Read the derivation and explore ↗Lynden-Bell & Pringle (1974) · The evolution of viscous discs ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Zel’dovich approximation and pancakes [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

First-order Lagrangian approximation
x(q,t) = q + D(t)s(q);　ρ/ρ̄ = 1/det(∂x/∂q)
Pressureless structure growth before shell crossing, in a single-stream description.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataSupply the initial displacement field and growth factor.Free parametersInitial displacement amplitude and shape, and growth factor.Validity and caveatsNot an exact solution of general three-dimensional gravitational evolution. Do not continue as a single stream after the Jacobian vanishes.Read the derivation and explore ↗Zel’dovich (1970) · Gravitational instability: an approximate theory ↗

Photon-balance equilibrium and an idealized front
Rₛ³ = 3QH/(4παB nₑnₚ)
Uniform pure hydrogen, ionization-bounded radiation, and case-B recombination.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataSupply the central ionizing-photon rate; at equilibrium it balances integrated recombinations.Free parametersPhoton rate, density, and recombination coefficient.Validity and caveatsOmit dust and photon leakage. Static ionization balance differs from later pressure-driven expansion.Read the derivation and explore ↗Strömgren (1939) · The physical state of interstellar hydrogen ↗

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Analytic Ornstein–Uhlenbeck closure
∂f/∂t = γ∂(vf)/∂v + D∂²f/∂v²
One-dimensional linear drift, constant diffusion, and an initially Gaussian distribution.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataVanishing flux at infinite velocity and unit total probability.Free parametersγ, D, and initial mean and variance.Validity and caveatsA benchmark, not a self-consistent cluster Fokker–Planck solution. Omits evolving gravity and transport coefficients.Read the derivation and explore ↗Cohn (1979) · Numerical integration of the Fokker–Planck equation ↗

## Lane–Emden equation and polytropes — Schwarzschild: clocks, the photon sphere, and the ISCO [en]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/en/solutions/
Coverage: reading

Exact vacuum Einstein solution
ds² = −(1−2rg/r)c²dt² + (1−2rg/r)−1dr² + r²dΩ²
Spherical vacuum, asymptotic flatness, and normalization by the central mass M.
Boundary data, parameters, and validityBoundary / initial dataApproach Minkowski spacetime far away and normalize t to a distant static clock.Free parametersM, with rg = GM/c². A test-particle orbit additionally needs conserved energy and angular momentum.Validity and caveatsExcludes stellar interiors and rotation. Distinguish the coordinate problem at 2rg from the curvature singularity at r = 0.Read the derivation and explore ↗Schwarzschild (1916) · Original paper, English translation ↗

Exact vacuum Einstein solution
χ = cJ/(GM²)
Stationary, axisymmetric, asymptotically flat rotating vacuum spacetime.

The panel uses a subextremal exterior; it does not integrate trajectories or render a shadow.Read the derivation and explore ↗Kerr (1963) · Gravitational field of a spinning mass ↗

本文35章と、全35題材の学習ガイドを公開しています。雲の平衡・重力不安定性・宇宙構造、球対称の降着と風、円盤の輸送・MRI・Toomre Q、流体境界、衝撃波、放射と電離を扱います。各題材に本文の公開状況を表示しています。

## 理論天文学の道具箱 — 番外編：宇宙物理の数字と単位 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/about/
Coverage: reading

pc、h・ℏ、電子の静止質量、太陽質量、H₀、微細構造定数、Gとcを参照できる番外編を用意しました。SIとCGS–Gauss系の対応を、数値と式の両方で確認できます。 数字と単位を開く ↗

## 理論天文学の道具箱 — この教材の先の教科書 [ja]

## 理論天文学の道具箱 — この教材の約束 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/about/
Coverage: reading

主役は天体現象です。状態方程式・不透明度・再結合係数などは意味と適用条件を示して使い、物性の微視的導出を独立の主題にはしません。
直感、仮定、式変形、確認問題をつなぎます。
近似、厳密な結果、既知の係数を区別し、適用条件を添えます。
発展編は「現象をつかむ・方程式を作る・解いて確かめる」の三段階で組み立てます。

## 理論天文学の道具箱 — 図の出典と検証 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/about/
Coverage: reading

掲載図は、現象を描いた概念図や理論模型を計算し直した教材図です。既知の解・保存則との検証と、特定の論文の図を再現することは区別します。論文の図との照合には、図番号、物理条件、軸と規格化、元データの範囲を明記します。

## 公開中の本文 — 星と雲の平衡、その限界 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

入門編のビリアル定理と白色矮星から、球とフィラメントの内部構造を解く段階へ。
つながる入門章：ビリアル定理 — 星は縮むと熱くなる ↗
01.1Lane–Emden方程式とポリトロープ 本文公開物理の問い
圧力と密度の関係を決めると、星の半径と質量はどう決まるか。
数学の道すじ
静水圧平衡と質量保存から無次元方程式へ。中心の正則条件、表面の零点、n＝3とチャンドラセカール質量をつなぐ。
本文を読む →
参考：Princeton · Polytropes ↗01.2Bonnor–Ebert球と外圧 本文公開物理の問い
同じ温度のガス雲を外から圧縮すると、どこで平衡が不安定になるか。
数学の道すじ
等温球の方程式を外圧で区切り、平衡系列をたどる。温度・外圧など、何を固定した安定性なのかを明示する。
本文を読む →
参考：Bonnor (1956) ↗01.3Ostrikerの等温円柱と限界線密度 本文公開物理の問い
細長いガスのフィラメントは、単位長さあたりどれだけの質量を支えられるか。
数学の道すじ
円柱のGauss則と静水圧平衡から解析解を作る。平らな中心、外側のr⁻⁴、線密度2cₛ²/G、外圧で切り取る場合と柱密度を区別する。J. P. Ostrikerを追悼する章。
本文を読む →
参考：Ostriker (1964), §III, equations (56)–(61) ↗01.4林限界と林トラック 本文公開物理の問い
重力収縮する前主系列星は、なぜほぼ一定の有効温度で暗くなるのか。
数学の道すじ
n＝3/2のポリトロープから完全対流の近似へ進み、光球の境界条件とH⁻の不透明度を結ぶ。L＝4πR²σT_eff⁴で収縮するトラックを読み、林限界の位置を決める大気物理と、進化の時間を決めるエネルギー収支を分ける。放射核ができる場合のHenyeyトラックとの違いも扱う。
本文を読む →
参考：Princeton · Hayashi limit ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 回転と非球対称の重力平衡 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

球を離れ、形と運動を方向ごとに考える。
つながる入門章：ビリアル定理 — 星は縮むと熱くなる ↗
02.1楕円体の重力ポテンシャル 本文公開物理の問い
同じ質量でも、潰したり伸ばしたりすると内部の重力はどう変わるか。
数学の道すじ
均質楕円体を入口に、ポテンシャルと軸比の関係を調べる。軸対称と三軸の形を比較する。
本文を読む →
参考：Binney · Dynamics of elliptical galaxies ↗02.2テンソルビリアルとMaclaurin／Jacobi系列 本文公開物理の問い
回転する流体はどんな形で平衡でき、その形は安定か。
数学の道すじ
方向ごとのつり合いから回転楕円体と三軸楕円体へ。平衡の存在、分岐、安定性を区別し、星の集団の平衡との違いも扱う。
本文を読む →
参考：Lebovitz (1961) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 重力不安定性と宇宙構造 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

背景と揺らぎを分け、何が成長するのかを問う。
つながる入門章：宇宙膨張とハッブル–ルメートルの法則 ↗
03.1Jeans不安定性とJeans swindle 本文公開物理の問い
圧力はどの波長の重力収縮を止められるか。一様な背景の重力をどう扱うか。
数学の道すじ
連続・運動・Poisson方程式を線形化して分散関係へ。静止した背景の仮定と、膨張宇宙での扱いを比較する。
本文を読む →
参考：Falco et al. · Why does the Jeans swindle work? ↗03.2Zel’dovich近似とパンケーキ 本文公開物理の問い
重力で集まる物質は、なぜ最初から球になるとは限らないのか。
数学の道すじ
ラグランジュ座標の写像と質量保存から密度を求め、一方向の圧縮を調べる。軌道交差で近似が破れる意味を説明する。
本文を読む →
参考：Zel’dovich (1970) ↗03.3Press–Schechter理論とハローの質量関数 本文公開物理の問い
初期の密度ゆらぎから、質量ごとのハローの個数をどう予測するか。
数学の道すじ
球対称崩壊で線形しきい値δcを求め、Gaussianの裾を質量割合へ対応させる。cloud-in-cloud問題と係数2を区別し、質量関数・べき乗模型の相似性・銀河形成への適用範囲を調べる。
本文を読む →
参考：Press & Schechter (1974) ↗03.4Lin–Mestel–Shu：一様楕円体の崩壊 本文公開物理の問い
圧力のない雲の小さな非球対称性は、崩壊中にどう育つか。
数学の道すじ
一様楕円体の内部重力を使い、質量保存と相似的な速度場から二つの半軸の運動方程式を導いて積分する。
本文を読む →
参考：Lin, Mestel & Shu (1965) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 流体境界の不安定性 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

境界の小さな揺れから、浮力・速度差による成長と混合の入口へ。
つながる入門章：星とガスと真空、宇宙を動かす力 ↗
04.1Rayleigh–Taylor不安定性 本文公開物理の問い
重い流体が軽い流体の上にあると、境界はどの速さで変形するか。
数学の道すじ
ポテンシャル流と境界の運動・圧力条件からω²＝−Agkへ。Atwood数、成長時間、短波長極限、非線形混合との違いを調べる。
本文を読む →
参考：Maryland · Hydrodynamics ↗04.2Kelvin–Helmholtz不安定性 本文公開物理の問い
境界に沿う速度差は、どうして波を育てるのか。
数学の道すじ
移流を含む境界条件から分散関係を導き、波の移動と成長を分ける。密度差・重力・有限の速度層の影響を比較する。
本文を読む →
参考：MIT · Interfacial phenomena ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 流体・相似解と高エネルギー放射 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/

爆風と重力崩壊を相似解で読み、衝撃波から粒子加速・放射へつなぐ。
つながる入門章：星の構造と、質量–光度関係 ↗
05.1Bondi降着と音速点 本文公開物理の問い
静かな周囲のガスは、どんな流れで中心の天体へ落ちるか。
数学の道すじ
定常・球対称の質量保存と運動方程式から、重力半径r_B＝GM/c_s,∞²と音速点を導く。等温模型をParker風と対にして読み、断熱模型では外側の密度・音速・比熱比から降着率が選ばれることを調べる。単なる自由落下の見積もりと臨界点を通る解を区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Bondi (1952) ↗05.2Parker風と超音速流出 本文公開物理の問い
熱い恒星大気は、どうやって重力に逆らい超音速で吹き出すか。
数学の道すじ
等温・球対称・定常流の風方程式(u−c_s²/u)du/dr＝2c_s²/r−GM/r²を導き、r_s＝GM/(2c_s²)で正則に音速を通る枝を選ぶ。Bondi降着との接続、境界条件、等温を保つ加熱が必要なこと、実際の太陽風の磁場との違いを扱う。
本文を読む →
参考：Parker (1958) ↗05.3Sedov–Taylor解とRankine–Hugoniot条件 本文公開物理の問い
放出エネルギーと周囲の密度から、爆風の半径はどう成長するか。
数学の道すじ
保存則から衝撃波の跳躍条件を導き、次元解析と相似変数で内部の速度・密度・圧力を解く。強い衝撃波、エネルギー保存などの条件を確認する。
本文を読む →
参考：Taylor (1950) ↗05.4Larson–Penston／Shuの等温崩壊 本文公開物理の問い
同じ等温ガスの方程式から、なぜ異なる崩壊解が選ばれるか。
数学の道すじ
球対称の流体方程式を相似変換し、音速点と初期・境界条件を調べる。両者を自動的な前後の進化段階として扱わない。
本文を読む →
参考：Shu (1977) ↗05.5衝撃波と粒子加速 本文公開物理の問い
衝撃波を往復する粒子は、なぜべき乗のエネルギー分布になるか。
数学の道すじ
Rankine–Hugoniot条件の圧縮比から、一次Fermi加速の増幅率と逃走確率を求める。加速時間と閉じ込め・冷却の限界を区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Maryland · Particle acceleration ↗05.6シンクロトロン放射と電子の冷却 本文公開物理の問い
加速電子の分布と磁場は、電波・X線のスペクトルにどう現れるか。
数学の道すじ
代表周波数・放射パワー・冷却時間を求め、電子のべき乗分布を光のスペクトルへ変換する。継続注入での冷却折れ曲がりを導く。
本文を読む →
参考：NRAO · Synchrotron radiation ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 星団の統計力学と緩和 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

一つの軌道から、分布とエネルギー輸送の進化へ。
つながる入門章：ビリアル定理 — 星は縮むと熱くなる ↗
06.12体緩和と重力散乱 本文公開物理の問い
小さな重力散乱が積み重なると、いつ軌道が大きく変わるか。
数学の道すじ
散乱の見積もりとランダムウォークから緩和時間へ。星の直接衝突や、孤立した二体軌道の問題とは区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Yale · Astrophysical flows ↗06.2Fokker–Planck方程式 本文公開物理の問い
遭遇による平均的な偏りとばらつきから、分布の変化をどう記述するか。
数学の道すじ
一変数の模型でドリフト・拡散を読み、星団の軌道平均と自己無撞着な重力場へ進む。分布の定義と保存則を確認する。
本文を読む →
参考：Cohn (1979) ↗06.3グラボサーマル・カタストロフィー 本文公開物理の問い
外側へエネルギーを渡す中心核が、さらに熱くなり収縮するのはなぜか。
数学の道すじ
負の熱容量と緩和による輸送をつなぐ。固定する熱力学的条件による安定性の違い、連星加熱、崩壊後の振動へ進む。
本文を読む →
参考：Lynden-Bell & Wood (1968) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 円盤の輸送と重力不安定性 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

角運動量輸送を入口に、降着・円盤風・α・MRI・自己重力をつなぐ。
つながる入門章：星とガスと真空、宇宙を動かす力 ↗
07.1角運動量輸送と降着 本文公開物理の問い
ガスが中心へ降着するには、角運動量をどこへ渡せばよいか。
数学の道すじ
円軌道のj＝√(GMr)から降着の条件を導き、二つの環で全角運動量と軌道エネルギーを確かめる。応力による半径方向の輸送と円盤風による持ち出し、定常円盤のトルク収支を区別してα・MRIへ接続する。
本文を読む →
参考：Princeton · Accretion disks ↗07.2薄い円盤とShakura–Sunyaevのαモデル 本文公開物理の問い
回転するガスが中心へ落ちるには、角運動量をどう運べばよいか。
数学の道すじ
鉛直平衡から厚みを見積もり、有効粘性ν＝αc_sHを導入。αが輸送効率のモデルであり、輸送の原因自体を決めないことを明示する。
本文を読む →
参考：Chambers (2009) · Viscous disk model ↗07.3Balbus–Hawley不安定性：MRI 本文公開物理の問い
流体力学的には安定な差動回転円盤が、弱い磁場で不安定になるのはなぜか。
数学の道すじ
磁力線の張力による角運動量交換から磁気回転不安定性を理解する。局所・軸対称・理想MHDの鉛直磁場で、Ωが外向きに減少する場合の分散関係と成長率を扱う。ケプラー回転の最大成長率3Ω/4を調べ、α輸送との接続、原始惑星系円盤の低電離での非理想MHDの必要性を明示する。
本文を読む →
参考：Balbus & Hawley (1991) ↗07.4Lynden–Bell–Pringleの粘性円盤相似解 本文公開物理の問い
物質は中心へ降着するのに、円盤の外側はなぜ広がるのか。
数学の道すじ
質量と角運動量の保存から面密度の進化方程式へ。粘性の半径依存性を指定して相似解を求め、降着と外向きの角運動量輸送を追う。
本文を読む →
参考：Lynden-Bell & Pringle (1974) ↗07.5Toomre Q、冷却、円盤の分裂 本文公開物理の問い
圧力と回転は自己重力に勝てるか。不安定な円盤は必ず分裂するか。
数学の道すじ
薄いガス円盤の局所分散関係からQを導く。軸対称の線形安定条件と、冷却を含む非線形の分裂条件を区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Gammie (2001) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 電離・散乱と天体ガスの観測 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

光とガスの相互作用から、電離領域の大きさと銀河団の圧力を測る。
つながる入門章：原子の光と星の光 — 10.2 eVの意味 ↗
08.1Strömgren球と電離・再結合 本文公開物理の問い
星の周りの水素ガスは、どこまで電離されて光るか。
数学の道すじ
一様密度・純水素・電離光子を閉じ込める静的模型で、Q_H＝(4π/3)R_S³α_B n_e n_pから電離半径を導く。再結合時間、case Bと局所再吸収、光子の逃走・塵吸収の仮定を確認し、初期の電離前線と後の圧力駆動膨張を静的平衡から区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Strömgren (1939) ↗08.2逆コンプトン散乱とSunyaev–Zel’dovich効果 本文公開物理の問い
銀河団の熱いガスは、宇宙背景放射をどう変え、何を教えてくれるか。
数学の道すじ
散乱光学的厚さと電子温度からyを求め、視線積分で電子圧力へ結ぶ。Kompaneets近似から熱的SZスペクトルと低周波の減光・約217 GHzの零点を求め、運動学的SZと相対論的補正の限界を区別する。
本文を読む →
参考：Carlstrom, Holder & Reese (2002) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 中性子星とパルサー磁気圏 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

回転と磁場から必要な電荷を数え、粒子加速と放射へつなぐ。
つながる入門章：古典的電子半径とトムソン散乱 ↗
09.1Goldreich–Julian密度と共回転 本文公開物理の問い
回転する中性子星の磁気圏には、どんな電荷密度が必要か。
数学の道すじ
共回転の電場E＝−(Ω×r)×Bとガウスの法則から、平坦時空・準静的で電流の項を省く近似のρ_GJ≈−2ε₀Ω·B（SI）を導く。n_GJ＝|ρ_GJ|/eは電荷数密度の尺度であり、電子・陽電子の総数密度とは区別する。光円柱、電荷不足と加速ギャップへの接続を扱い、この近似を磁気圏全体に適用しない。
本文を読む →
参考：Goldreich & Julian (1969) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 非平衡・多成分の宇宙物理 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

温度、磁場と中性ガス、化学組成が同じ速さで変わるとは限らない。緩和・拡散・反応の時間を天体の運動と比較する。
つながる入門章：星とガスと真空、宇宙を動かす力 ↗
10.12温度プラズマと電子・イオンの緩和 本文公開物理の問い
同じガスの電子とイオンが、違う温度を持てるのはなぜか。
数学の道すじ
熱容量とエネルギー保存から温度交換を導く。Coulomb交換時間を入力して緩和を追い、流れの時間と比較する。
本文を読む →
参考：Vink et al. (2015) ↗10.2両極性拡散：磁場と中性ガスのずれ 本文公開物理の問い
低電離の雲では、磁場とガスはいつ一緒に動かなくなるか。
数学の道すじ
Lorentz力とイオン・中性粒子の摩擦をつり合わせ、拡散係数と時間を導く。線形の磁場の凹凸を減衰させ、重力の時間と比べる。
本文を読む →
参考：Hillier (2024) ↗10.33体化学反応と水素分子の形成 本文公開物理の問い
密度が高くなると、3体反応で水素分子が作られやすくなるのはなぜか。
数学の道すじ
H＋H＋H → H₂＋Hの粒子数を数え、原子核保存と反応率から分子化の解析解を導く。量子化学の係数は与え、反応時間と重力時間を比べる。
本文を読む →
参考：Forrey (2013), equation (6) ↗

## 公開中の本文 — 相対論の時空と軌道 [ja]

Source: https://koyamahr.github.io/astronomy-step-by-step/ja/advanced/
Coverage: reading

計量を出発点として、時計・光・粒子の運動を式で調べる。
つながる入門章：一般相対論への入口 — 光と時計と重力 ↗
11.1Schwarzschild解：時計・光子球・ISCO 本文公開物理の問い
同じ時空の中で、地平面・光子球・最内安定円軌道はどう違うか。

本文を読む →
参考：Carroll · General Relativity, §7 ↗11.2Kerr解：回転・エルゴ領域・ISCO 本文公開物理の問い

計量から角速度の許容範囲を導き、赤道面の半径ポテンシャルから順行・逆行のISCOを調べる。
本文を読む →
参考：Bardeen, Press & Teukolsky (1972) ↗11.3富松–佐藤解：Ernstポテンシャルと四重極 本文公開物理の問い
同じ質量と角運動量でも、Kerrとは違う真空時空を作れるか。
数学の道すじ
δ＝2のErnstポテンシャルを入力とし、軸上の展開から質量・角運動量・四重極を読み、環状特異点の条件を調べる。
